A unified framework for notions of algebraic theory The author is supported by JST ERATO HASUO Metamathematics for Systems Design Project (No. JPMJER1603) and the JSPS-INRIA Bilateral Joint Research Project “CRECOGI”.
Soichiro Fujii

Abstract
Universal algebra uniformly captures various algebraic structures, by expressing them as equational theories or abstract clones. The ubiquity of algebraic structures in mathematics and related fields has given rise to several variants of universal algebra, such as theories of symmetric operads, non-symmetric operads, generalised operads, PROPs, PROs, and monads. These variants of universal algebra are called notions of algebraic theory. In this paper, we develop a unified framework for them. The key observation is that each notion of algebraic theory can be identified with a monoidal category, in such a way that algebraic theories correspond to monoid objects therein. To incorporate semantics, we introduce a categorical structure called metamodel, which formalises a definition of models of algebraic theories. We also define morphisms between notions of algebraic theory, which are a monoidal version of profunctors. Every strong monoidal functor gives rise to an adjoint pair of such morphisms, and provides a uniform method to establish isomorphisms between categories of models in different notions of algebraic theory. A general structure-semantics adjointness result and a double categorical universal property of categories of models are also shown.



keywords
Algebraic theories, clones, operads, double limits

AMS
18C10, 18D05

1
Introduction

Algebras permeate both pure and applied mathematics.
Important types of algebras, such as vector spaces, groups and rings, arise
naturally in many branches of mathematical sciences and
it would hardly be an exaggeration to say that algebraic structures
are one of the most universal and fundamental structures in mathematics.

A type of algebras, such as groups, is normally specified by a family of
operations and a family of equational axioms.
We call such a specification of a type of algebras an
algebraic theory
,
and call a background theory for a type of algebraic theories a
notion of
algebraic theory
.
In order to capture various types of algebras, a variety of notions of
algebraic theory have been introduced.
Examples include universal algebra
[
Bir35
]
,
symmetric and non-symmetric operads
[
May72
]
,
generalised operads (also called clubs)

[
Bur71
,
Kel92
,
Her00
,
Lei04
]
,
PROPs and PROs
[
ML65
]
,
and monads
[
EM65
,
Lin66
]
;
we shall review these notions of algebraic theory in
Section
3
.

Notions of algebraic theory all aim to provide a means to define algebras, but
they attain this goal in quite distinct manners.
The diversity of the existing notions of algebraic theory
leaves one to wonder what, if any, is a formal core or essence shared by them.
Our main aim in this paper is to provide an answer to this
question, by developing a unified
framework for notions of algebraic theory.

The starting point of our approach is quite simple.
We identify a notion of algebraic theory with an (arbitrary) monoidal category,
and algebraic theories in a notion of algebraic theory with monoid objects in
the corresponding monoidal category.
As we shall see in Section
4
,
it has been observed (or easily follows from known observations)
that each type of algebraic theories we have
listed above can be characterised as monoid objects in a suitable monoidal
category.
From now on let us adopt the terminology to be introduced in
Section
4
:
we call a monoidal category a
metatheory
and a monoid object therein
a
theory
, to remind ourselves of our intention.

In order to formalise the semantical aspect of notions of algebraic theory—by
which we mean definitions of
models
(= algebras) of an algebraic
theory, their homomorphisms, and so on—we introduce the concept of

metamodel
.
Metamodels are a certain categorical structure defined relative to
a metatheory
\mathcal{M}
and a category
\mathcal{C}
,
and are meant to capture a

notion of model
of an algebraic theory, i.e., what it means to take a
model of a theory in
\mathcal{M}

in the category
\mathcal{C}
.
A model of an algebraic theory is always given relative to some notion of
model, even though usually it is not recognised explicitly.
We shall say more about the idea of notions of model at the beginning of
Section
5
.
A metamodel of a metatheory
\mathcal{M}
in a category
\mathcal{C}

generalises both an
\mathcal{M}
-category (as in enriched category theory) whose underlying category is
\mathcal{C}
,
and a (left) oplax action of
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{C}
.
Indeed, as we shall see in Sections
5
and

6
, it has been observed that enrichments
(which we introduce as a slight variant of
\mathcal{M}
-categories)
and oplax actions can account for the standard semantics of the known
notions of algebraic theory.
Our concept of metamodel provides a unified account of the semantical aspects
of notions of algebraic theory.

Metamodels of a fixed metatheory
\mathcal{M}
naturally form a
2-category
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}
,
and we shall see that theories in
\mathcal{M}
can be identified with
certain metamodels of
\mathcal{M}
in the terminal category
1
.
This way we obtain a fully faithful 2-functor
from the category
{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}
of theories in
\mathcal{M}
(which is just the
category of monoid objects in
\mathcal{M}
)
to
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}
.
A metamodel
\Phi
of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{C}
provides a definition of model of
a theory in
\mathcal{M}
as an object of
\mathcal{C}
with additional
structure,
hence if we fix a metamodel
(\mathcal{C},\Phi)
and a theory
\mathsf{T}
,
we obtain the
category of models
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
equipped with
the forgetful functor

U\colon{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
.
By exploiting the 2-category
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}
, the
construction
{\mathbf{Mod}(-,-)}
of categories of models may be expressed as
the following composition

{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}
\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
,
inclusion
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}(-,-)

(1)

where
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}(-,-)
is the hom-2-functor
and
\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
is a 2-category of categories.

We also introduce morphisms (and 2-cells) between metatheories
(Section
8
).
Such morphisms are a monoidal version of profunctors.
The principal motivation of the introduction of morphisms of metatheories is
to compare different notions of algebraic theory,
and indeed our morphisms of metatheories induce 2-functors between the
corresponding 2-categories of metamodels.
Analogously to the well-known fact for profunctors
that any functor induces an adjoint pair of profunctors,
we see that any strong monoidal functor
F
induces an adjoint pair

F_{\ast}\dashv F^{\ast}
of morphisms of metatheories.
Therefore, whenever we have a strong monoidal functor

F\colon\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}
between metamodels,
we obtain a 2-adjunction

{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{N})}
.
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(F_{\ast})}
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(F^{\ast})}
\dashv

(2)

Now, the strong monoidal functor
F
also induces a functor

{\mathbf{Th}(F)}\colon{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{N})},

which is in fact a restriction of
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(F_{\ast})}
.
This implies that, immediately from the description (
1
)
of categories of models and the 2-adjointness (
2
),
for any
\mathsf{T}\in{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}
and
(\mathcal{C},\Phi)\in{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{N})}
,
we have a canonical isomorphism of categories

{\mathbf{Mod}({\mathbf{Th}(F)}(\mathsf{T}),(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}\cong{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(F^{\ast})}(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}.

(3)

In fact, as we shall see, the action of
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(-)}
on morphisms of metatheories
preserves the “underlying categories” of metamodels.
So
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(F^{\ast})}(\mathcal{C},\Phi)
is a metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{C}
,
and we have an isomorphism of categories
over

\mathcal{C}

(that is, the isomorphism (
3
) commutes with the forgetful
functors).

The above argument gives a unified conceptual account for a range of known
results on the compatibility of semantics of notions of algebraic theory.
For example, it is known that any clone (or Lawvere theory)
\mathsf{T}

induces a monad
\mathsf{T}^{\prime}
on
\mathbf{Set}

in such a way that the models of
\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{T^{\prime}}
in
\mathbf{Set}

(with respect to the standard notions of model) coincide;
this result follows from the existence of a natural strong monoidal functor
between the metatheories corresponding to clones and monads on
\mathbf{Set}
,
together with a simple observation that the induced 2-functor between
the 2-categories of metamodels preserves the standard metamodel.
This and other examples will be treated in
Section
8
.

In Section
9
we study
structure-semantics adjunctions

within our framework.
If we fix a metatheory
\mathcal{M}
and a metamodel
(\mathcal{C},\Phi)
of
\mathcal{M}
,
we obtain a functor

{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}

(4)

by mapping a theory
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{M}
to the category of models

{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
equipped with the forgetful functor
into
\mathcal{C}
.
The functor (
4
) is sometimes called the

semantics functor
,
and it has been observed for many notions of algebraic theory that
this functor (or an appropriate variant of it) admits a left adjoint
called the
structure functor

[
Law63
,
Lin66
,
Lin69
,
Dub70
,
Str72
,
Ave17
]
.
The idea behind the structure functor is as follows.
One can regard a functor
V\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

into
\mathcal{C}
as specifying an additional structure (in a very broad sense) on
objects in
\mathcal{C}
, by viewing
\mathcal{A}
as the category of
\mathcal{C}
-objects
equipped with that structure, and
V
as the forgetful functor.
The structure functor then maps
V
to the best approximation of that structure
by theories in
\mathcal{M}
.
Indeed, if (
4
) is fully faithful (though
this is not always the case), then the structure functor reconstructs the
theory from
its category of models.

We cannot get a left adjoint to the functor
(
4
) for an arbitrary metatheory
\mathcal{M}
and its
metamodel
(\mathcal{C},\Phi)
.
In order to get general structure-semantics adjunctions,
we extend the category
{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}
of theories in
\mathcal{M}

to the category
{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}
of theories in the metatheory

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=[\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{SET}]
equipped with the convolution
monoidal structure
[
Day70
]
.
We show in Theorem
69
that
the structure-semantics adjunction

{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}^{\mathrm{op}}
{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}
\mathrm{Str}
\mathrm{Sem}
\vdash

exists for any metatheory
\mathcal{M}
and its metamodel
(\mathcal{C},\Phi)
.

We conclude the paper in Section
10
,
by giving a universal characterisation of categories of models in our
framework.
It is well-known that the Eilenberg–Moore categories (= categories of models)
of monads can be characterised by a 2-categorical universal property in the
2-category
\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
of categories
[
Str72
]
.
We show in Theorem
73

that our category of models admit a similar universal characterisation,
but instead of inside the 2-category
\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
, inside the
pseudo double
category

{\mathbb{PROF}}
of categories, functors, profunctors and natural
transformations.
The notion of pseudo double category, as well as
{\mathbb{PROF}}
itself,
was introduced by Grandis and Paré
[
GP99
]
.
In the same paper they also introduced the notion of
double limit
,
a suitable limit notion in (pseudo) double categories.
The double categorical universal property that our categories of models
enjoy can also be formulated in terms of double limits;
see Corollary
74
.

During the investigation of our framework, we have encountered a number of
problems, several of which are still open.
A major open problem is that of characterising via intrinsic properties
the forgetful functors from the categories of models
arising in our framework.
In the case of monads, the corresponding result is the various

monadicity theorems
, such as Beck’s theorem

[
ML98
, Section VI.7]
.
We shall discuss this problem further at the end of
Section
8
.

2
Set theoretic conventions

Due to the metatheoretical nature of the subject matter, in this paper
we will use multiple (Grothendieck)
universes
;
see e.g.,
[
ML98
, Section I.6]
or
[
KS05
, Definition 1.1.1]

for definitions of universes.

For the purpose of this paper, we assume
the existence of three universes
\mathcal{U}_{1}
,

\mathcal{U}_{2}
and
\mathcal{U}_{3}
with
\mathcal{U}_{1}\in\mathcal{U}_{2}\in\mathcal{U}_{3}
.
We now fix these universes once and for all.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a universe.
We define size-regulating conditions
on sets and other mathematical structures
in reference to
\mathcal{U}
.

•

A set is said to be

in
\mathcal{U}
if
it is an element of
\mathcal{U}
.

In this paper, a category is always assumed to have sets of objects
and of morphisms (rather than
proper classes
of them).
We say that a category
\mathcal{C}
is

•

in
\mathcal{U}

if the tuple
({\mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})},(\mathcal{C}(A,B))_{A,B\in{\mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})}},({\mathrm{id}_{C}}\in\mathcal{C}(C,C))_{C\in{\mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})}},(\circ_{A,B,C}\colon

\mathcal{C}(B,C)\times\mathcal{C}(A,B)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(A,C))_{A,B,C\in{\mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})}})
, consisting of the data for
\mathcal{C}
,
is an element of
\mathcal{U}
;

•

locally in
\mathcal{U}

if for each
A,B\in{\mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})}
,
the hom-set
\mathcal{C}(A,B)
is in
\mathcal{U}
.

We also write
C\in\mathcal{C}
for
C\in{\mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})}
.

We extend these definitions to other mathematical structures.
For example, a group is said to be
in
\mathcal{U}
if it
(i.e., the tuple consisting of its data) is an element
of
\mathcal{U}
,
a 2-category is
locally in
\mathcal{U}

if all its hom-categories are in
\mathcal{U}
, and so on.

Recall the universes
\mathcal{U}_{1}
,
\mathcal{U}_{2}
and
\mathcal{U}_{3}

we have fixed above.

Definition 1
.

A set or other mathematical structure (group, category, etc.)
is said to be:

•

small
if it is in
\mathcal{U}_{1}
;

•

large
if it is in
\mathcal{U}_{2}
;

•

huge
if it is in
\mathcal{U}_{3}
.

Sets and other mathematical structures are often assumed to be small by
default, even when we do not say so explicitly.

A category (or a 2-category) is said to be:

•

locally small
if it is large and locally in
\mathcal{U}_{1}
;

•

locally large
if it is huge and locally in
\mathcal{U}_{2}
.

In the following, we mainly talk about the
size-regulating conditions using the terms
small
,
large

and
huge
,
avoiding direct references to the universes
\mathcal{U}_{1}
,
\mathcal{U}_{2}

and
\mathcal{U}_{3}
.

We shall use the following basic (2-)categories throughout this paper.

•

\mathbf{Set}
, the (large) category of all small sets and functions.

•

\mathbf{SET}
, the (huge) category of all large sets and functions.

•

\mathbf{Cat}
, the (large) category of all small categories and functors.

•

{\mathbf{CAT}}
, the (huge) category of all large categories and functors.

•

{\mathscr{C}\!at}
, the (large) 2-category of all small categories, functors and
natural
transformations.

•

\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
, the (huge) 2-category of all large categories, functors and
natural
transformations.

•

{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}
, the (huge) 2-category of all large monoidal categories, lax
monoidal functors
1
1

1

Also called
monoidal functors
in e.g.,

[
ML98
]
.
and monoidal natural transformations.
We also use several variants of it.

•

\underline{2\mathchar 45\relax\mathscr{C\!A\!T}}
, the 2-category of all huge 2-categories,
2-functors and 2-natural transformations.

3
Notions of algebraic theory

In this section, we review several known
notions of algebraic theory.
As we shall see later, they all turn out to be instances of our unified
framework for notions of algebraic theory developed from
the next section on.
A more introductory account of these notions of algebraic theory
(except for PROPs and PROs) may be found in
[
Fuj18
, Chapter 2]
.

3.1
Clones

Abstract clones
(
clones
for short)
[
Tay93
]
are a
presentation independent
version of equational theories in universal algebra and, as such,
they are more or less equivalent to
Lawvere
theories

[
Law63
]
.
Let us begin with the definition.

Definition 2
.

A
clone

\mathsf{T}
consists of:

(CD1)

a family of sets
T=(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
indexed by natural numbers;

(CD2)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, an element

p^{(n)}_{i}\in T_{n};

(CD3)

for each
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
, a function

\circ^{(n)}_{k}\colon T_{k}\times(T_{n})^{k}\longrightarrow T_{n}

whose action on an element
(\phi,\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})\in T_{k}\times(T_{n})^{k}

we write as
\phi\circ^{(n)}_{k}(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})

or simply
\phi\circ(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})
;

satisfying the following equations:

(CA1)

for each
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
,

\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k}\in T_{n}
,

p^{(k)}_{j}\circ^{(n)}_{k}(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})=\theta_{j};

(CA2)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\theta\in T_{n}
,

\theta\circ^{(n)}_{n}(p^{(n)}_{1},\dots,p^{(n)}_{n})=\theta;

(CA3)

for each
l,k,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\psi\in T_{l}
,
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{l}\in T_{k}
,
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k}\in T_{n}
,

\psi\circ^{(k)}_{l}\big(\phi_{1}\circ^{(n)}_{k}(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k}),\ \dots,\ \phi_{l}\circ^{(n)}_{k}(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})\big)\\
=\big(\psi\circ^{(k)}_{l}(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{l})\big)\circ^{(n)}_{k}(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k}).

Such a clone is written as

\mathsf{T}=(T,(p^{(i)}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N},i\in\{1,\dots,n\}},(\circ^{(n)}_{k})_{k,n\in\mathbb{N}})

or simply
(T,p,\circ)
.

The following example shows a typical way in which clones arise.

Example 3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small category with all finite products,
and
C
be an object of
\mathcal{C}
.
Then we obtain the clone
\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)=({\langle C,C\rangle},p,\circ)
defined as
follows:

(CD1)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
{\langle C,C\rangle}_{n}
be the hom-set

\mathcal{C}(C^{n},C)
, where
C^{n}
is the product of
n
copies of
C
(the

n
-th power of
C
);

(CD2)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, let

p^{(n)}_{i}\colon C^{n}\longrightarrow C
be the
i
-th projection;

(CD3)

for each
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
g\colon C^{k}\longrightarrow C

and
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\colon C^{n}\longrightarrow C
, let

g\circ^{(n)}_{k}(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})\colon

C^{n}\longrightarrow C
be the following
composite in
\mathcal{C}
:

C^{n}
C^{k}
C.
\langle f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\rangle
g

It is straightforward to check the axioms (CA1)–(CA3).

In fact,
every
clone arises in the above manner.
To see this, let
\mathsf{T}=(T,p,\circ)
be an arbitrary clone.
We can then define the category
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{T}}
,
whose set of objects is the set
\mathbb{N}
of natural numbers and
whose hom-sets are given by
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{T}}(n,m)=(T_{n})^{m}
.
We may routinely define identity morphisms and composition in

\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{T}}
from the structure of
\mathsf{T}
,
and it turns out that the object
n\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{T}}

is the
n
-th power of
1\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{T}}
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{T}}
;
thus we recover
\mathsf{T}
as
\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{T}}}(1)
.
This construction also shows how clones and Lawvere theories are
related.
See
[
Tay93
]
for details.

Another source of clones is provided by

presentations of equational theories

in universal algebra.
A presentation of an equational theory
{{\langle\,\Sigma\,|\,E\,\rangle}}

is given by a family
\Sigma
of basic operations (with designated arities)
and a family
E
of equational axioms between
\Sigma
-terms.
Given such a data, we can define a clone

\mathsf{T}^{{\langle\,\Sigma\,|\,E\,\rangle}}=(T^{{\langle\,\Sigma\,|\,E\,\rangle}},p,\circ)

by setting
(T^{{\langle\,\Sigma\,|\,E\,\rangle}})_{n}
to be the set of all
\Sigma
-terms
in which at most
n
different variables occur, modulo
equational
theorems
derivable from
E
,
p
to be the equivalence classes
of variables, and
\circ
to be the
simultaneous substitution; see
[
Fuj18
, Sections 2.1 and 2.2]
for
details.
It is well-known that various types of algebras, such as groups, monoids and
rings, admit presentations of equational theories, and hence by the above
construction we obtain the
clone of groups
, etc.
Such are the motivating examples of clones
qua
algebraic theories.

Homomorphisms of clones may be defined routinely.

Definition 4
.

Let
\mathsf{T}=(T,p,\circ)
and
\mathsf{T^{\prime}}=(T^{\prime},p^{\prime},\circ^{\prime})
be
clones.
A
clone homomorphism
from
\mathsf{T}
to
\mathsf{T^{\prime}}

is a family of functions

h=(h_{n}\colon T_{n}\longrightarrow T^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
which preserves the structure
of clones;
precisely,

•

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
h_{n}(p^{(n)}_{i})=p^{\prime(n)}_{i}
;

•

for each
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\phi\in T_{k}
and
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k}\in T_{n}
,

h_{n}\big(\phi\circ^{(n)}_{k}(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})\big)=h_{k}(\phi)\circ^{\prime(n)}_{k}\big(h_{n}(\theta_{1}),\dots,h_{n}(\theta_{k})\big).

Let us turn to the definition of models of a clone.
One can consider models of a clone in any locally small category with finite
products.

Definition 5
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a clone and
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small category with all
finite products.
A
model of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
consists of an object

C\in\mathcal{C}
together with
a clone homomorphism
\chi\colon\mathsf{T}\longrightarrow\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)
.

For example, models of the clone of groups in
\mathcal{C}
are precisely

group objects
(or
internal groups
) in
\mathcal{C}
.

We then define the notion of homomorphism between models (of a clone).
First we note the functoriality of the
{\langle-,-\rangle}
construction,
already appeared (partly) in Example
3
.
For the current purpose, it suffices to remark that for each locally small
category
\mathcal{C}
with finite products and each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
the assignment
{\langle A,B\rangle}_{n}=\mathcal{C}(A^{n},B)
canonically extends to a
functor

{\langle-,-\rangle}_{n}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}.

Definition 6
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a clone,
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small category with all
finite products, and
(A,\alpha)
and
(B,\beta)
be models of

\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
.
A
homomorphism
from
(A,\alpha)
to
(B,\beta)
is a morphism

f\colon A\longrightarrow B
in
\mathcal{C}
such that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
the following diagram commutes:

T_{n}
{\langle A,A\rangle}_{n}
{\langle B,B\rangle}_{n}
{\langle A,B\rangle}_{n}.
\alpha_{n}
{\langle A,f\rangle}_{n}
\beta_{n}
{\langle f,B\rangle}_{n}

For each clone
\mathsf{T}
and a locally small
category
\mathcal{C}
with all finite products, we denote the category of all
models of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
and their homomorphisms by

{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}
.
Note that we have the canonical forgetful functor

U\colon{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
.

3.2
Non-symmetric operads

Non-symmetric operads
[
May72
]
may be seen as a variant
of clones.
Compared to clones, non-symmetric operads are less expressive (for example,
groups cannot be captured by non-symmetric operads), but
their models can be taken in wider contexts than for clones (whereas models of
clones are taken in a locally small category with finite products,
models of non-symmetric operads can be taken in any locally small monoidal
category).

Definition 7
.

A
non-symmetric operad

\mathsf{T}
consists of:

(ND1)

a family of sets
T=(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
indexed by natural numbers;

(ND2)

an element
{\mathrm{id}}\in T_{1}
;

(ND3)

for each
k,n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, a function
(we omit the sub- and superscripts)

\circ\colon T_{k}\times T_{n_{1}}\times\dots\times T_{n_{k}}\longrightarrow T_{n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}}

whose action we write as
(\phi,\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})\longmapsto\phi\circ(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{k})

satisfying the following equations:

(NA1)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta\in T_{n}
,

{\mathrm{id}}\circ(\theta)=\theta;

(NA2)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta\in T_{n}
,

\theta\circ({\mathrm{id}},\dots,{\mathrm{id}})=\theta;

(NA3)

for each
l,\ k_{1},\dots,k_{l},\ n_{1,1},\dots,n_{1,k_{1}},\dots,n_{l,1},\dots,n_{l,k_{l}}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\psi\in T_{l}
,
\phi_{1}\in T_{k_{1}},\dots,\phi_{l}\in T_{k_{l}}
,
\theta_{1,1}\in T_{n_{1,1}},\dots,\theta_{1,k_{1}}\in T_{n_{1,k_{1}}},\dots,\theta_{l,1}\in T_{n_{l,1}},\dots,

\theta_{l,k_{l}}\in T_{n_{l,k_{l}}}
,

\psi\circ\big(\phi_{1}\circ(\theta_{1,1},\dots,\theta_{1,k_{1}}),\ \dots\ ,\phi_{l}\circ(\theta_{l,1},\dots,\theta_{l,k_{l}})\big)\\
=\big(\psi\circ(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{l})\big)\circ(\theta_{1,1},\dots,\theta_{1,k_{1}},\ \dots,\ \theta_{l,1},\dots,\theta_{l,k_{l}}).

Such a non-symmetric operad is written as
\mathsf{T}=(T,{\mathrm{id}},\circ)
.

Example 8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C},I,\otimes)
be a locally small monoidal category,
and
C
be an object of
\mathcal{C}
.
Define the non-symmetric operad
\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)=({\langle C,C\rangle},{\mathrm{id}},\circ)

as follows:

(ND1)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
{\langle C,C\rangle}_{n}
be the hom-set

\mathcal{C}(C^{\otimes n},C)
, where
C^{\otimes n}
is the monoidal product of

n
copies of
C
;

(ND2)

let

{\mathrm{id}}\colon C\longrightarrow C
be the identity morphism on
\mathcal{C}
;

(ND3)

for each
k,n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
,
g\colon C^{\otimes k}\longrightarrow C

and
f_{1}\colon C^{\otimes n_{1}}\longrightarrow C,\dots,f_{k}\colon C^{\otimes n_{k}}\longrightarrow C
, let

g\circ(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})\colon

C^{\otimes(n_{1}+\dots+n_{k})}\longrightarrow C

be
the following
composite in
\mathcal{C}
:

C^{\otimes(n_{1}+\dots+n_{k})}\cong C^{\otimes n_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes C^{\otimes n_{k}}
C^{\otimes k}
C.
f_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes f_{k}
g

It is straightforward to check the axioms (NA1)–(NA3).

Just like the case of clones, it can be shown that every non-symmetric operad
arises as above.
Given an arbitrary non-symmetric operad
\mathsf{T}=(T,{\mathrm{id}},\circ)
,
we define a strict monoidal category
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{T}}

whose set of objects is
\mathbb{N}
and whose hom-sets are defined as

\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{T}}(n,m)=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{m}\in\mathbb{N}\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{m}=n\end{subarray}}T_{n_{1}}\times\dots\times T_{n_{m}},

with sum of natural numbers as the monoidal product;
cf.
[
Lei04
, Section 2.3]
.
We then obtain
\mathsf{T}
as
\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{T}}}(1)
.

As for examples of non-symmetric operads naturally seen as algebraic theories,
we may obtain a non-symmetric operad
from a strongly regular presentation of an equational theory.
Here, a presentation of an equational theory is called
strongly
regular
if
each of its equational axioms satisfies the condition that, on each side of the
equation, exactly same variables appear without repetition and in the same
order.
For example, assuming that
e
is a nullary operation and
\cdot
is a binary
operation, the equations

(x\cdot y)\cdot z=x\cdot(y\cdot z),\quad x\cdot e=x

are strongly regular, whereas the equations

x\cdot x=x,\quad x\cdot y=x,\quad x\cdot y=y\cdot x

are not.
The structure of monoids can be expressed by a non-symmetric operad.

We may define the notion of
non-symmetric operad homomorphism

between non-symmetric operads
just in the same way as that of clone homomorphism
(Definition
4
).

The definition of models of a non-symmetric operad is also similar to that of
a clone (Definition
5
).

Definition 9
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a non-symmetric operad and
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small
monoidal category.
A
model of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
consists of an object

C\in\mathcal{C}
together with a non-symmetric operad homomorphism

\chi\colon\mathsf{T}\longrightarrow\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)
.

With the definition of homomorphisms between models
completely parallel to the case of clones (Definition
6
),
we obtain the category of models
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}

(as well as the associated forgetful functor)
for each non-symmetric operad
\mathsf{T}
and a locally small monoidal
category
\mathcal{C}
.

3.3
Symmetric operads

Symmetric operads
[
May72
]
are an intermediate notion of algebraic theory
which lie between clones and
non-symmetric operads, both in terms of expressive power and
in terms of range of notions of models.

In terms of expressive power, symmetric operads correspond to
regular presentations of equational theories,
where a presentation of an equational theory is called
regular

if each of its equational axioms satisfies the following condition:
on each side of the equation, exactly same variables appear without repetition
(but possibly in different order).
So we may express
commutative
monoids in addition to monoids
via symmetric operads.

In terms of range of notions of models,
one can take models of a symmetric operad in any locally small symmetric
monoidal category.

We omit the definitions of symmetric operads, their models and so on,
which are analogous to the cases of clones and non-symmetric operads.
See e.g.
[
Fuj18
, Section 2.4]
for details.

3.4
PROs

PROs
[
ML65
]
are a certain generalisation of
non-symmetric operads.

Definition 10
.

A
PRO
is a small strict monoidal category
whose set of objects is
\mathbb{N}
and whose monoidal product
on objects is the sum of natural numbers.

Homomorphisms of PROs are identity-on-objects strict monoidal functors.

The relationship of PROs to non-symmetric operads is as follows.
Given a non-symmetric operad, one can define a PRO
by the construction sketched just after Example
8
.
PROs are more general than non-symmetric operads in that algebraic structures
described by them allow many-in, many-out operations
(operations of type
C^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow C^{\otimes m}
), whereas
those described by non-symmetric operads
allow only many-in, single-out operations (those of type
C^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow C
).
Therefore via PROs, one can capture the structure of

comonoids
in addition to monoids.

Example 11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C},I,\otimes)
be a locally small monoidal category,
and
C
be an object of
\mathcal{C}
.
Then we obtain the PRO
\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)
defined as follows:
for each
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
, let the hom-set
\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)(n,m)
of
the PRO be the hom-set

\mathcal{C}(C^{\otimes n},C^{\otimes m})
of
\mathcal{C}
, with the rest of the
structure
induced from
\mathcal{C}
in the evident manner.

Models of a PRO
\mathsf{T}

can be taken in any locally small monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
,
and are sometimes defined as strong monoidal functors of type

\mathsf{T}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
,
following the doctrine of
functorial semantics

[
Law63
]
.
We shall adopt the following alternative, more or less equivalent definition
(appearing e.g., in
[
ML65
]
).

Definition 12
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a PRO and
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small monoidal category.
A
model of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
consists of
an object
C\in\mathcal{C}
together with a PRO homomorphism

\chi\colon\mathsf{T}\longrightarrow\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)
.

With the straightforward definition of homomorphisms of models
(just like the case of clones), we obtain
the category of models
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}
and
the associated forgetful functor.

The above definition of models not only looks similar to the corresponding
definitions for clones, symmetric operads and non-symmetric operads,
but it also eliminates certain redundancy involved in
the definition of models as certain functors.
That is, if we define a model to be a strong monoidal functor

\mathsf{T}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
,
to give such a data
we have to specify the image of each object in
\mathsf{T}
,
and this involves a choice, for each natural number
n
,
of a specific
n
-fold monoidal product of the
underlying object; but
such choices are usually not included
in the data of an algebraic structure.
In more precise terms,
such a definition of models
(in the spirit of functorial semantics) in general prevents
the forgetful functor out of the category of models from being

amnestic

[
AHS06
, Definition 3.27]
.
On the other hand, it will turn out that
all forgetful functors arising in our unified framework for notions of
algebraic theory are amnestic.

3.5
PROPs

PROPs
[
ML65
]
are the symmetric version of PROs.

Definition 13
.

A
PROP
is a small symmetric strict monoidal category whose set of
objects is
\mathbb{N}
and whose monoidal product on objects is the sum
of natural numbers.

Here, by a
symmetric strict monoidal category
we mean
a symmetric monoidal category
whose structure isomorphisms
except for the symmetry
are
identities.
Similarly to the case of PROs, we can
take models of a PROP in any locally small symmetric monoidal category.

3.6
Monads

The definition of monads on a category is well-known.
For any large category
\mathcal{C}
, we shall denote the category of
all monads on
\mathcal{C}
and monad morphisms by
{\mathbf{Mnd}(\mathcal{C})}
,
which we define to be the category of monoid objects
in the monoidal category
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]
of all endofunctors on
\mathcal{C}

(with composition of endofunctors as monoidal product);
note that our definition of monad morphism is opposite
in the direction to the one defined in
[
Str72
]
.

The following definition of
models of a monad, usually called
Eilenberg–Moore
algebras
, is also well-known.

Definition 14

(
[
EM65
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a large category and
\mathsf{T}=(T,\eta,\mu)
be a monad on

\mathcal{C}
.

1.

An
Eilenberg–Moore algebra of
\mathsf{T}

consists of:

•

an object
C\in\mathcal{C}
;

•

a morphism
\gamma\colon TC\longrightarrow C
in
\mathcal{C}
,

making the following diagrams commute:

C
TC
C
\eta_{C}
\gamma
{\mathrm{id}_{C}}

TTC
TC
TC
C.
\mu_{C}
\gamma
T\gamma
\gamma

2.

Let
(C,\gamma)
and
(C^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
be Eilenberg–Moore algebras of

\mathsf{T}
.
A
homomorphism
from
(C,\gamma)
to
(C^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})

is a morphism
f\colon C\longrightarrow C^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}

making the following diagram commute:

TC
TC^{\prime}
C
C^{\prime}.
Tf
\gamma^{\prime}
\gamma
f

The category of all Eilenberg–Moore algebras of
\mathsf{T}
and their
homomorphisms is called the
Eilenberg–Moore category of
\mathsf{T}
,
and is denoted by
\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}}
.

Excellent introductions to monads abound (see e.g.,

[
ML98
, Chapter VI]
).
Here we simply remark that, seen as a notion of algebraic theory, monads on

\mathbf{Set}
have stronger expressive power than
clones, and can express such infinitary structures as
compact Hausdorff spaces and complete semilattices
[
Man76
]
.

Let us make clear our convention as to which class of monads counts as a
single notion of algebraic theory.
We understand that for each large category
\mathcal{C}
,
the monads on
\mathcal{C}
form a single notion of algebraic theory.
So actually we have just introduced
an infinite family of notions of algebraic theory, one notion
for each large category.

3.7
Generalised operads

Just like monads are a family of notions of algebraic theory
parameterised by a large category, the term

generalised operads

[
Bur71
,
Kel92
,
Her00
,
Lei04
]

also refer to a family of notions of algebraic
theory, this time parameterised by a large category with finite limits and a

cartesian monad
thereon.
We start with the definition of cartesian monad.

Definition 15
.

1.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be categories, and
F,G\colon\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}
be functors.
A natural transformation
\alpha\colon F\longrightarrow G

is called
cartesian
if and only if all naturality squares of
\alpha

are pullback squares;
that is, if and only if
for any morphism
f\colon C\longrightarrow C^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
,
the square

FC
GC
FC^{\prime}
GC^{\prime}
\alpha_{C}
Gf
Ff
\alpha_{C^{\prime}}

is a pullback of
Gf
and
\alpha_{C^{\prime}}
.

2.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with all pullbacks.
A monad
\mathsf{S}=(S,\eta,\mu)
on
\mathcal{C}
is called

cartesian
if and only if the functor
S
preserves pullbacks,
and
\eta
and
\mu
are cartesian.

For each cartesian monad
\mathsf{S}=(S,\eta,\mu)
on a large category
\mathcal{C}

with all finite limits
we now introduce
\mathsf{S}
-operads
, which
form a single notion of algebraic theory.

The crucial observation is that under this assumption,
the slice category
\mathcal{C}/S1
(where
1
is the terminal object of
\mathcal{C}
)
acquires a canonical monoidal structure, given as follows.
(We write an object of
\mathcal{C}/S1
either as
p\colon P\longrightarrow S1

or
(P,p)
.)

•

The unit object is
I=(\eta_{1}\colon 1\longrightarrow S1)
.

•

Given a pair of objects
p\colon P\longrightarrow S1
and
q\colon Q\longrightarrow S1
in
\mathcal{C}/S1
,
first form the pullback

(Q,q)\ast P
SP
Q
S1,
\pi_{2}
S!
\pi_{1}
q

(5)

where
!\colon P\longrightarrow 1
is the unique morphism to the terminal
object.
The monoidal product
(Q,q)\otimes(P,p)\in\mathcal{C}/S1

is
((Q,q)\ast P,\mu_{1}\circ Sp\circ\pi_{2})
:

(Q,q)\ast P
SP
SS1
S1.
\pi_{2}
Sp
\mu_{1}

We remark that this monoidal category arises as a restriction of Burroni’s
bicategory of
\mathsf{S}
-spans
[
Bur71
]
.

Definition 16
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a large category with all finite limits
and
\mathsf{S}=(S,\eta,\mu)
a cartesian monad on
\mathcal{C}
.

1.

An
\mathsf{S}
-operad
is a monoid object in the monoidal
category

(\mathcal{C}/S1,I,\otimes)
introduced above.

2.

A
morphism of
\mathsf{S}
-operads
is a homomorphism
of monoid objects in
(\mathcal{C}/S1,I,\otimes)
.

We denote the category of
\mathsf{S}
-operads and their homomorphisms by

{{\mathsf{S}}\mathchar 45\relax\mathbf{Opd}}
;
by definition it is identical to the category of monoid
objects in
\mathcal{C}/S1
.

Given an
\mathsf{S}
-operad
\mathsf{T}=(({\mathrm{ar}_{T}}\colon T\longrightarrow S1),e,m)
, we may interpret
the object
S1
in
\mathcal{C}

as the object of arities,
T
as the object of all
(derived) operations of the algebraic theory expressed by
\mathsf{T}
,
and
{\mathrm{ar}_{T}}
as assigning an arity to each operation.

Example 17

(
[
Lei04
, Example 4.2.7]
)
.

If we let
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{Set}
and
\mathsf{S}
be the free monoid monad
(which is cartesian),
then
\mathsf{S}
-operads are equivalent to non-symmetric operads.
The arities are the natural numbers:
S1\cong\mathbb{N}
.

In more detail, the data of an
\mathsf{S}
-operad in this case
consist of a set
T
, and functions

{\mathrm{ar}_{T}}\colon T\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}
,
e\colon 1\longrightarrow T

and
m\colon(T,{\mathrm{ar}_{T}})\ast T\longrightarrow T
.
Unravelling this, we obtain a family of sets
(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
an element
{\mathrm{id}}\in T_{1}
and
a family of functions
(m_{k,n_{1},\dots n_{k}}\colon T_{k}\times T_{n_{1}}\times\dots\times T_{n_{k}}\longrightarrow T_{n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}})_{k,n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}}
,
agreeing with Definition
7
.
Note that indeed
T_{n}
may be interpreted as the set of all (derived)
operations of arity
n
.

Example 18
.

If we set
\mathcal{C}={{n}\mathchar 45\relax\mathbf{Gph}}
, the category of
n
-graphs for

n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}

and
\mathsf{S}
be the free strict
n
-category monad,
then

\mathsf{S}
-operads are called
n
-globular operads
;
see
[
Lei04
, Chapter 8]
for details.
These generalised operads have been used to give a definition of
weak
n
-categories
[
Bat98
,
Lei04
]
.

Next we define models of an
\mathsf{S}
-operad.
For this, we first note that the monoidal category
\mathcal{C}/S1
has a canonical
pseudo action on
\mathcal{C}
.
(The precise definition of pseudo action is a variant of
Definition
45
, obtained by replacing the term “natural
transformation” there by “natural isomorphism”.)
The functor

\ast\colon(\mathcal{C}/S1)\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

defining this pseudo action is given by mapping
((Q,q),P)\in(\mathcal{C}/S1)\times\mathcal{C}
to

(Q,q)\ast P\in\mathcal{C}
defined as the pullback (
5
).

Definition 19
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a large category with finite limits,
\mathsf{S}=(S,\eta,\mu)

be a cartesian monad on
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathsf{T}=(T,e,m)
be an

\mathsf{S}
-operad.

1.

A
model of
\mathsf{T}
consists of:

•

an object
C\in\mathcal{C}
;

•

a morphism
\gamma\colon T\ast C\longrightarrow C
in
\mathcal{C}
,

making the following diagrams commute:

I\ast C
T\ast C
{C}

\gamma
\cong

\gamma

where the arrows labelled with
\cong
refer to the isomorphisms
provided by the pseudo action.

2.

Let
(C,\gamma)
and
(C^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
be models of
\mathsf{T}
. A

homomorphism from
(C,\gamma)
to

(C^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})

is a morphism
f\colon C\longrightarrow C^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
making the
following diagram commute:

T\ast C
T\ast C^{\prime}
C
C^{\prime}.
\gamma
T\ast f
{f}
\gamma^{\prime}

We saw in Example
17

that non-symmetric operads arise as a special case of generalised operads.
In fact the definitions of models also agree,
the models defined by the above definition (where
\mathsf{S}
is taken as in
Example
17
) being equivalent to the models as in
Definition
9
, with
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{Set}

equipped with the cartesian monoidal structure.

4
Metatheories and theories

In the previous section
we have seen several examples of notions of algebraic theory,
in which the corresponding types of algebraic theories are called
under various names, such as
clones
,
non-symmetric operads
and

monads (on
\mathcal{C}
)
.
Being a background theory for a type of algebraic theories,
each notion of algebraic theory has definitions of algebraic theory,
of model of an algebraic theory,
and of homomorphism between models.
Nevertheless, different notions of algebraic theory take
different approaches to define these concepts, and the
resulting definitions (say, of algebraic theory) can look quite remote.

As we have already mentioned, this paper introduces a

unified framework for notions of algebraic theory

which includes all of the notions
of algebraic theory reviewed in the previous section
as instances.
To the best of our knowledge, this is the first framework for notions of
algebraic theory attaining such generality.
Due to the diversity of notions of algebraic theory
we aim to capture, we take a very simple approach:
the basic idea is that we identify
notions of algebraic theory

with (large)
monoidal categories
,
and
algebraic theories
with
monoid objects
therein.

Definition 20
.

A
metatheory
is a large
monoidal category
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
.

Metatheories are intended to formalise notions of algebraic theory.
We remark that, in this paper,
we leave the term
notion of algebraic theory
informal
and will not give any mathematical definitions to it.

Definition 21
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a metatheory.
A
theory in
\mathcal{M}
is a monoid object

\mathsf{T}=(T,e,m)
in
\mathcal{M}
.

We denote the category of theories in
\mathcal{M}
by
{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}
,
which we define to be the same as
the category of monoid objects in
\mathcal{M}
and homomorphisms between them.

Theories formalise what we have been calling
algebraic theories
.

The above definitions simply introduce aliases to well-known concepts.
Our hope is that, by using the terms which reflect our intention,
statements and discussions become easier to follow;
think of the terms such as
generalised element
(which is synonymous
to morphism in a category) or
map
(used by some authors to mean
left adjoint in a bicategory)
which have been used with great benefit in the literature.

4.1
The metatheory
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
for clones

As a first example, we treat clones (Definition
2
) within
our framework.
That is, we seek a suitable metatheory
for which clones are theories therein.

Definition 22
.

Let
\mathbf{F}
be the category defined as follows:

•

The set of objects is
{\mathrm{ob}(\mathbf{F})}=\{\,[n]\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\,\}
,
where for each natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
[n]
is
defined to be the
n
-element set
\{1,\dots,n\}
.

•

A morphism is any function between these sets.

So the category
\mathbf{F}
is a skeleton of the category
\mathbf{FinSet}

of all (small) finite sets and functions.
The underlying category of the metatheory for clones
is the category
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
of
all functors from
\mathbf{F}
to
\mathbf{Set}
and natural transformations.
For
X\in[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
and
[n]\in\mathbf{F}
,
we write the set
X([n])
as
X_{n}
.

The monoidal structure on the category
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]

we shall consider is known as the
substitution monoidal
structure
.

Definition 23

(
[
KP93
,
FPT99
]
)
.

The
substitution monoidal structure
on
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
is defined as
follows:

•

The unit object is
I=\mathbf{F}([1],-)\colon\mathbf{F}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
.

•

Given
X,Y\colon\mathbf{F}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
, their monoidal product

Y\otimes X\colon\mathbf{F}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
maps
[n]\in\mathbf{F}
to

(Y\otimes X)_{n}=\int^{[k]\in\mathbf{F}}Y_{k}\times(X_{n})^{k}.

(6)

The integral sign with a superscript in
(
6
) stands for a
coend

(dually, we will denote an
end
by the integral sign with a subscript);
see
[
ML98
, Section IX. 6]
.

This defines a metatheory
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
.
We claim that clones correspond to
theories in
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
.
A theory in
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
consists of

•

a functor
T\colon\mathbf{F}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
;

•

a natural transformation
e\colon I\longrightarrow T
;

•

a natural transformation
m\colon T\otimes T\longrightarrow T

satisfying the monoid axioms.
By the Yoneda lemma,
e
corresponds to an element
\overline{e}\in T_{1}
,
and by the universality of coends,
m
corresponds to
a natural transformation
2
2

2

In more detail, the relevant naturality
here may also be phrased as
“natural in
[n]\in\mathbf{F}
and
extranatural
in
[k]\in\mathbf{F}
”;
see
[
ML98
, Section IX. 4]
.
Following
[
Kel82
]
, in this paper we shall not distinguish
(terminologically)
extranaturality from naturality, using the latter term for both.

(\overline{m}_{n,k}\colon T_{k}\times(T_{n})^{k}\longrightarrow T_{n})_{n,k\in\mathbb{N}}.

Hence given a theory
(T,e,m)
in
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
, we can construct a
clone with the underlying family of sets
(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
by setting

p^{(n)}_{i}=T_{\lceil i\rceil}(\overline{e})
(here,

{\lceil i\rceil}\colon[1]\longrightarrow[n]

is the morphism in
\mathbf{F}
defined as
{\lceil i\rceil}(1)=i
) and

\circ^{(n)}_{k}=\overline{m}_{n,k}
.
Conversely, given a clone
(T,p,\circ)
,
we can construct a theory in
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
as follows.
First we extend the family of sets
T

to a functor
T\colon\mathbf{F}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}

by setting, for any
u\colon[m]\longrightarrow[n]
in
\mathbf{F}
,

T_{u}(\theta)=\theta\circ^{(n)}_{m}(p^{(m)}_{u(1)},\dots,p^{(m)}_{u(m)}).

Then we may set
\overline{e}=p^{(1)}_{1}
and
\overline{m}_{n,k}=\circ^{(n)}_{k}
.

Proposition 24

(Cf.
[
Cur12
,
Hyl14
]
)
.

The above constructions establish an isomorphism of categories
between the category of clones and
{\mathbf{Th}([\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}])}
.

4.2
The metatheory
[\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}]
for symmetric operads

Symmetric operads can be similarly
seen as theories in a suitable metatheory.
The main difference from the case of clones is that,
instead of the category
\mathbf{F}
, we use the following category.

Definition 25
.

Let
\mathbf{P}
be the category defined as follows:

•

The set of objects is the same as
\mathbf{F}
:
{\mathrm{ob}(\mathbf{P})}=\{\,[n]\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\,\}
where
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
.

•

A morphism is any
bijective
function.

So
\mathbf{P}
is the subcategory of
\mathbf{F}
consisting of all isomorphisms.

Symmetric operads are
theories in a metatheory whose underlying category is
the functor category
[\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}]
.
The monoidal structure on
[\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}]
we shall use is
also called the substitution monoidal structure.

Definition 26

(
[
Kel05
]
)
.

The
substitution monoidal structure
on
[\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}]
is defined as
follows:

•

The unit object is
I=\mathbf{P}([1],-)\colon\mathbf{P}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
.

•

Given
X,Y\colon\mathbf{P}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
, their monoidal product

Y\otimes X\colon\mathbf{P}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
maps
[n]\in\mathbf{P}
to

(Y\otimes X)_{n}=\int^{[k]\in\mathbf{P}}Y_{k}\times(X^{\circledast k})_{n},

where

(X^{\circledast k})_{n}=\int^{[n_{1}],\dots,[n_{k}]\in\mathbf{P}}\mathbf{P}([n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}],[n])\times X_{n_{1}}\times\dots\times X_{n_{k}}.

Proposition 27

(Cf.
[
Cur12
,
Hyl14
]
)
.

The category of symmetric operads is isomorphic to
{\mathbf{Th}([\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}])}
.

4.3
The metatheory
[\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}]
for non-symmetric operads

For non-symmetric operads (Definition
7
),
we use the following category.

Definition 28
.

Let
\mathbf{N}
be the category defined as follows:

•

The set of objects is the same as
\mathbf{F}
and
\mathbf{P}
.

•

There are only identity morphisms in
\mathbf{N}
.

\mathbf{N}
is the discrete category with the same objects as
\mathbf{F}
and
\mathbf{P}
.
We again consider the functor category
[\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}]
.

Definition 29
.

The
substitution monoidal structure
on
[\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}]
is defined as
follows:

•

The unit object is
I=\mathbf{N}([1],-)\colon\mathbf{N}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
.

•

Given
X,Y\colon\mathbf{N}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
, their monoidal product

Y\otimes X\colon\mathbf{N}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
maps
[n]\in\mathbf{N}
to

(Y\otimes X)_{n}=\coprod_{[k]\in\mathbf{N}}Y_{k}\times\bigg(\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}[n_{1}],\dots,[n_{k}]\in\mathbf{N}\\
n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}=n\end{subarray}}X_{n_{1}}\times\dots\times X_{n_{k}}\bigg).

Proposition 30

(Cf.
[
Cur12
,
Hyl14
]
)
.

The category of non-symmetric operads is isomorphic to
{\mathbf{Th}([\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}])}
.

For unified studies of various substitution monoidal structures,
see
[
TP06
,
FGHW18
]
, as well as the
aforementioned
[
Cur12
,
Hyl14
]
.

4.4
The metatheory
{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}(\mathbf{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set})
for PROs

In order to characterise PROs as theories in a metatheory,
we combine the following known facts.

•

A PRO (Definition
10
) can be
given equivalently as a small monoidal category
\mathsf{T}
equipped with
an identity-on-objects strict monoidal functor from
\mathbf{N}

(Definition
28
; the monoidal structure on
\mathbf{N}
is given by
sum of natural numbers)
to
\mathsf{T}
.

•

Fix a small category
\mathcal{C}
.
A pair
(\mathcal{D},J)
consisting of a small category

\mathcal{D}
(having the same set of objects as
\mathcal{C}
)
and an identity-on-objects functor

J\colon\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}

is given equivalently as a monad on
\mathcal{C}
in the bicategory of
(
\mathbf{Set}
-valued) profunctors,
that is to say as a monoid in the monoidal category of endo-profunctors on

\mathcal{C}
.

Hence it is natural to consider a monoidal version of endo-profunctors on

\mathbf{N}
.
Since the notion of profunctor (and its monoidal variant) will recur in
this paper, let us fix our notation at this point.
As we shall later be more concerned with
\mathbf{SET}
-valued profunctors
than
\mathbf{Set}
-valued ones (cf. Section
2
),
we consider the former as default.

Definition 31

(
[
Bén00
,
Law73
]
)
.

We define the bicategory
{\mathscr{P\!R\!O\!F}}
as follows.

•

An object is a large category.

•

A 1-cell from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
is a
profunctor from

\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
, which we define to be
a functor

H\colon\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}.

We write
H\colon\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{B}
if
H
is a profunctor from
\mathcal{A}

to
\mathcal{B}
.
The identity 1-cell on a large category
\mathcal{C}

is the hom-functor
\mathcal{C}(-,-)
.
Given profunctors
H\colon\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{B}
and
K\colon\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{C}
,
their composite

K\odot H\colon\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{C}
maps
(C,A)\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{A}

to

(K\odot H)(C,A)=\int^{B\in\mathcal{B}}K(C,B)\times H(B,A).

(7)

•

A 2-cell from
H
to
H^{\prime}
, both from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
,
is a natural transformation
\alpha\colon H\Longrightarrow H^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
.

Between small categories we also consider
\mathbf{Set}
-valued
profunctors
, defined by replacing
\mathbf{SET}
by
\mathbf{Set}
in the above definition of
profunctors.

Definition 32

(Cf.
[
IK86
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I_{\mathcal{M}},\otimes_{\mathcal{M}})
and

\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{N},I_{\mathcal{N}},\otimes_{\mathcal{N}})
be large monoidal categories.
A
monoidal profunctor
from
\mathcal{M}
to

\mathcal{N}
is a lax monoidal functor

H=(H,h_{\cdot},h)\colon\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET},

where
\mathbf{SET}
is regarded as a monoidal category
via the cartesian monoidal structure.
In detail, it consists of:

•

a functor
H\colon\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
;

•

a function
h_{\cdot}\colon 1\longrightarrow H(I_{\mathcal{N}},I_{\mathcal{M}})
,
where
1
is a singleton;

•

a natural transformation

h=(h_{N,N^{\prime},M,M^{\prime}}\colon H(N^{\prime},M^{\prime})\times H(N,M)\longrightarrow H(N^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{N}}N,M^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{M}}M))_{N,N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N},M,M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}}

satisfying the suitable coherence axioms.

One can compose monoidal profunctors by the formula
(
7
), giving rise to the bicategory of
large monoidal categories, monoidal profunctors, and monoidal natural
transformations;
we shall use this in Section
8
.
Between small monoidal categories, one can define

\mathbf{Set}
-valued monoidal profunctors
and their compositions.
Corresponding to the aforementioned fact about profunctors,
it can be shown that for any small monoidal category
\mathcal{M}
,
to give a monad on
\mathcal{M}
in the bicategory of
\mathbf{Set}
-valued monoidal
profunctors
is equivalent to give a small monoidal category
\mathcal{N}
(having the same set
of objects as
\mathcal{M}
) together with an identity-on-objects

strict
monoidal functor
J\colon\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}
.
It follows that PROs correspond to theories in the metatheory of

\mathbf{Set}
-valued monoidal endo-profunctors on
\mathbf{N}
, whose underlying category
is
{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}(\mathbf{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set})
, the category of lax monoidal
functors of type
\mathbf{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{N}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
and monoidal
natural transformations.

4.5
The metatheory
{\mathscr{S}\!ym\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}(\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set})
for PROPs

The case of PROPs is a straightforward adaptation of that of PROs.
For small symmetric monoidal categories
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
,
we define a
\mathbf{Set}
-valued
symmetric monoidal profunctor
from
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathcal{N}

to be a lax symmetric monoidal functor of type

\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}.

Similarly to
\mathbf{Set}
-valued monoidal profunctors,

\mathbf{Set}
-valued symmetric monoidal profunctors compose and
form a bicategory, a monad therein (say, on
\mathcal{M}
) being equivalent to
a small symmetric monoidal category
equipped with an identity-on-objects strict symmetric monoidal functor
from
\mathcal{M}
.

We can endow the category
\mathbf{P}
(Definition
25
)
with the natural structure of a symmetric strict monoidal category.
As a PROP (Definition
13
) can be defined equivalently as a small
symmetric monoidal category equipped with an identity-on-objects
strict symmetric monoidal functor from
\mathbf{P}
,
we conclude that PROPs correspond to theories in
the metatheory of
\mathbf{Set}
-valued symmetric monoidal endo-profunctors
on
\mathbf{P}
, whose underlying category is

{\mathscr{S}\!ym\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}(\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set})
,
the category of lax symmetric monoidal functors of type

\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{P}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
and monoidal natural transformations.

4.6
The metatheories for monads and generalised operads

Finally, we recall that monads
and generalised operads were introduced as monoid objects in the first place.
Hence for a large category
\mathcal{C}
,
the metatheory for monads on
\mathcal{C}
is
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]
(with the
composition as monoidal product), whereas
for a large category
\mathcal{C}
with finite limits and a cartesian monad

\mathsf{S}
thereon, the metatheory for
\mathsf{S}
-operads is
\mathcal{C}/S1

(with the monoidal structure defined earlier).

5
Notions of model as enrichments

Let us proceed to incorporate semantical aspects of notions of algebraic theory
into our framework.
We start with a discussion on
notions of model
.
An important feature of several notions of algebraic
theory—especially clones, symmetric operads, non-symmetric
operads, PROPs and PROs—is that we may consider
models of an algebraic theory in more than one category.
For example,
models of a clone can be taken in any locally small category with
finite products (Definition
5
).
We may phrase this fact by saying that clones admit multiple notions of
model, one for each locally small category with finite products.

Informally, a
notion of model
for a notion of algebraic theory
is a definition of model of an algebraic theory in that notion of algebraic
theory.
Hence whenever we consider actual
models
of an algebraic theory, we
must specify in advance a notion of model with respect to which the models are
taken.
Our framework emphasises the inevitable fact that

models are always relative to notions of model
,
by treating notions of model explicitly as mathematical structures.

But how can we formalise such notions of model?
Below we show that the standard notions of model for clones, symmetric operads,
non-symmetric operads, PROPs and PROs can be captured by a categorical structure
which we call
enrichment
.

Definition 33

(Cf.
[
Cam18
, Definition 2.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
be a metatheory.
An
enrichment over
\mathcal{M}
consists of:

•

a large category
\mathcal{C}
;

•

a functor

{\langle-,-\rangle}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}
;

•

a natural transformation
(j_{C}\colon I\longrightarrow{\langle C,C\rangle})_{C\in\mathcal{C}}
;

•

a natural transformation

(M_{A,B,C}\colon{\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}\longrightarrow{\langle A,C\rangle})_{A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}}
,

making the following diagrams commute for all
A,B,C,D\in\mathcal{C}
:

I\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}
{\langle B,B\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}
{\langle A,B\rangle}
j_{B}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}
M_{A,B,B}
\cong

{\langle A,B\rangle}\otimes I
{\langle A,B\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,A\rangle}
{\langle A,B\rangle}
{\langle A,B\rangle}\otimes j_{A}
M_{A,A,B}
\cong

({\langle C,D\rangle}\otimes{\langle B,C\rangle})\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}
{\langle B,D\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}
{\langle C,D\rangle}\otimes({\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle})
{\langle C,D\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,C\rangle}
{\langle A,D\rangle}.
M_{B,C,D}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}
M_{A,B,D}
\cong
{\langle C,D\rangle}\otimes M_{A,B,C}
M_{A,C,D}

We say that
(\mathcal{C},{\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
is an enrichment over
\mathcal{M}
,
or that
({\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
is an enrichment of
\mathcal{C}
over
\mathcal{M}
.

An enrichment over
\mathcal{M}
is
not the same as a (large)
\mathcal{M}
-category
in enriched category
theory
[
Kel82
]
.
It is rather a triple consisting of a large category
\mathcal{C}
,
a large
\mathcal{M}
-category
\mathcal{D}

and an identity-on-objects functor
J\colon\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}
,
where
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is the underlying category of
\mathcal{D}
.

In more detail, given an enrichment
({\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
of
\mathcal{C}
in

\mathcal{M}
,
we may define the
\mathcal{M}
-category
\mathcal{D}
with
{\mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{D})}={\mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{C})}

using the data
({\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
of the enrichment, by setting

\mathcal{D}(A,B)={\langle A,B\rangle}
and so on.
The identity-on-objects functor
J\colon\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}

may be defined by mapping a morphism
f\colon A\longrightarrow B

in
\mathcal{C}
to
the composite
{\langle A,f\rangle}\circ j_{A}
, or equivalently,
{\langle f,B\rangle}\circ j_{B}
:

I
{\langle B,B\rangle}
{\langle A,A\rangle}
{\langle A,B\rangle}.
j_{A}
j_{B}
{\langle A,f\rangle}
{\langle f,B\rangle}

See
[
Cam18
, Section 2]
and
[
Fuj18
, Section 3.1.2]
for more about
the relationship to enriched category theory.

From an enrichment, we now derive a definition of model
of a theory.
First observe that, given an enrichment
{\langle-,-\rangle}=({\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
of
a large category
\mathcal{C}
over a metatheory
\mathcal{M}

and an object
C\in\mathcal{C}
,
we have a theory
\mathsf{End}_{{\langle-,-\rangle}}(C)=({\langle C,C\rangle},j_{C},M_{C,C,C})

in
\mathcal{M}
.

Definition 34
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
be a metatheory,

\mathsf{T}=(T,e,m)
be a theory in
\mathcal{M}
,

\mathcal{C}
be a large category, and
{\langle-,-\rangle}=({\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
be an
enrichment of
\mathcal{C}
over
\mathcal{M}
.

1.

A
model of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
with respect to

{\langle-,-\rangle}

is a pair
(C,\chi)
consisting of an object
C
of
\mathcal{C}

and a theory homomorphism (= monoid homomorphism)
\chi\colon\mathsf{T}\longrightarrow\mathsf{End}_{\langle-,-\rangle}(C)
; that is,
a morphism
\chi\colon T\longrightarrow{\langle C,C\rangle}

in
\mathcal{M}
making the following diagrams commute:

I
T
{\langle C,C\rangle}
e
\chi
j_{C}

T\otimes T
T
{\langle C,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle C,C\rangle}
{\langle C,C\rangle}.
\chi\otimes\chi
m
M_{C,C,C}
\chi

2.

Let
(C,\chi)
and
(C^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})
be models of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}

with respect to
{\langle-,-\rangle}
. A
homomorphism from
(C,\chi)
to

(C^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})

is a morphism
f\colon C\longrightarrow C^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
making the
following diagram commute:

T
{\langle C^{\prime},C^{\prime}\rangle}
{\langle C,C\rangle}
{\langle C,C^{\prime}\rangle}.
\chi
\chi^{\prime}
{\langle C,f\rangle}
{\langle f,C^{\prime}\rangle}

We denote the (large) category of models of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
with
respect to

{\langle-,-\rangle}
by
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},{\langle-,-\rangle}))}
.

The above definitions of model and homomorphism are reminiscent of
ones for clones (Definitions
5
and
6
),
symmetric operads, non-symmetric operads
(Definition
9
),
PROPs and PROs (Definition
12
).
Indeed, we can recover the standard notions of model
for these notions of algebraic theory via suitable enrichments.

Example 35
.

Recall that the metatheory for clones is
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
with the substitution
monoidal structure.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small
3
3

3

Note that according to
our convention (Definition
1
), “locally small” implies
“large”.

category with all finite products
(or more generally, with all finite powers).
We have an enrichment of
\mathcal{C}
over
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
defined as follows:

•

The functor
{\langle-,-\rangle}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]

maps
A,B\in\mathcal{C}
and
[n]\in\mathbf{F}
to the set

{\langle A,B\rangle}_{n}=\mathcal{C}(A^{n},B).

•

The natural transformation
(j_{C}\colon I\longrightarrow{\langle C,C\rangle})_{C\in\mathcal{C}}
corresponds by the Yoneda lemma
(recall that
I=\mathbf{F}([1],-)
) to the family

(\overline{j}_{C}={\mathrm{id}_{C}}\in{\langle C,C\rangle}_{1})_{C\in\mathcal{C}}.

•

The natural transformation
(M_{A,B,C}\colon{\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}\longrightarrow{\langle A,C\rangle})_{A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}}

corresponds by the universality of coends (recall
that
(Y\otimes X)_{n}=\int^{[k]\in\mathbf{F}}Y_{k}\times(X_{n})^{k}
) to
the family

(\overline{M}_{A,B,C})_{n,k}\colon{\langle B,C\rangle}_{k}\times({\langle A,B\rangle}_{n})^{k}\longrightarrow{\langle A,B\rangle}_{n}

mapping
(g,f_{1},\dots,f_{k})\in\mathcal{C}(B^{k},C)\times\mathcal{C}(A^{n},B)^{k}

to
g\circ\langle f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\rangle\in\mathcal{C}(A^{n},B)
.

Clearly the definition of the clone
\mathsf{End}_{\mathcal{C}}(C)
from an object

C\in\mathcal{C}

(Definition
3
) is derived from the above enrichment.
Consequently, we recover the classical definitions of model
(Definition
5
)
and homomorphism between models (Definition
6
) for clones,
as instances of Definition
34
.

Example 36
.

The metatheory for symmetric operads is
[\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}]
with the substitution
monoidal structure.
Let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C},I^{\prime},\otimes^{\prime})

be a locally small symmetric monoidal category.
We have an enrichment of
\mathcal{C}
over
[\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}]
defined as follows:

•

The functor
{\langle-,-\rangle}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow[\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}]

maps
A,B\in\mathcal{C}
and
[n]\in\mathbf{P}
to the set

{\langle A,B\rangle}_{n}=\mathcal{C}(A^{\otimes^{\prime}n},B),

where
A^{\otimes^{\prime}n}
is the monoidal product of
n
many copies of
A
.

•

The natural transformation
(j_{C}\colon I\longrightarrow{\langle C,C\rangle})_{C\in\mathcal{C}}
corresponds by the Yoneda lemma
(recall that
I=\mathbf{P}([1],-)
) to the family

(\overline{j}_{C}={\mathrm{id}_{C}}\in{\langle C,C\rangle}_{1})_{C\in\mathcal{C}}.

•

The natural transformation
(M_{A,B,C}\colon{\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}\longrightarrow{\langle A,C\rangle})_{A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}}

corresponds by the universality of coends (recall
Definition
26
) to
the family

(\overline{M}_{A,B,C})_{n,k,n_{1},\dots,n_{k}}\colon{\langle B,C\rangle}_{k}\times\mathbf{P}([n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}],[n])\\
\times{\langle A,B\rangle}_{n_{1}}\times\dots\times{\langle A,B\rangle}_{n_{k}}\longrightarrow{\langle A,B\rangle}_{n},

which is the unique function from the empty set if
n\neq n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}

and, if
n=n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}
, maps

(g,u,f_{1},\dots,f_{k})\in\mathcal{C}(B^{\otimes^{\prime}k},C)\times\mathbf{P}([n_{1}+\dots+n_{k}],[n])\times\mathcal{C}(A^{\otimes^{\prime}n_{1}},B)\times\dots\times\mathcal{C}(A^{\otimes^{\prime}n_{k}},B)

to
g\circ(f_{1}\otimes^{\prime}\dots\otimes^{\prime}f_{k})\circ A^{\otimes^{\prime}u}
.

Via the above enrichment, we recover the classical definitions of model and
homomorphism between models for symmetric operads.

Example 37
.

The metatheory for non-symmetric operads is
[\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}]
with the
substitution monoidal structure.
Let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C},I^{\prime},\otimes^{\prime})

be a locally small monoidal category.
We have an enrichment of
\mathcal{C}
over
[\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}]
which is
similar to, and simpler than, the one in the previous example.

Example 38
.

The metatheory for PROPs is
{\mathscr{S}\!ym\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}(\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set})

with the monoidal structure given by composition of
\mathbf{Set}
-valued
symmetric monoidal profunctors.
Let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C},I^{\prime},\otimes^{\prime})
be a locally small symmetric monoidal
category.
We have an enrichment of
\mathcal{C}
over
{\mathscr{S}\!ym\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}(\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set})

defined as follows:

•

The functor
{\langle-,-\rangle}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}\!ym\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}(\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set})

maps
A,B\in\mathcal{C}
and
[m],[n]\in\mathbf{P}
to the set

{\langle A,B\rangle}([m],[n])=\mathcal{C}(A^{\otimes^{\prime}n},B^{\otimes^{\prime}m}),

with the evident lax symmetric monoidal structure
on
{\langle A,B\rangle}
.

•

The natural transformation
(j_{C}\colon\mathbf{P}(-,-)\longrightarrow{\langle C,C\rangle})
is determined by the functoriality of
{\langle C,C\rangle}
.

•

The natural transformation
(M_{A,B,C}\colon{\langle B,C\rangle}\odot{\langle A,B\rangle}\longrightarrow{\langle A,C\rangle})_{A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}}

corresponds by the universality of coends to the family

(\overline{M}_{A,B,C})_{l,m,n}\colon{\langle B,C\rangle}([l],[m])\times{\langle A,B\rangle}([m],[n])\longrightarrow{\langle A,C\rangle}([l],[n])

defined by composition in
\mathcal{C}
.

This enrichment captures the classical definition of models of PROPs.

Example 39
.

The metatheory for PROs is
{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}(\mathbf{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set})
with the
monoidal structure given by composition of
\mathbf{Set}
-valued monoidal profunctors.
Let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C},I^{\prime},\otimes^{\prime})

be a locally small monoidal category.
We have an enrichment of
\mathcal{C}
over
{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}(\mathbf{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set})

which is similar to, and simpler than, the one in the previous example.

Example 40
.

We may also consider infinitary variants of Example
35
.
Here we take an extreme.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small category with all small powers.
Then we obtain an enrichment of
\mathcal{C}
over
[\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{Set}]
,
the metatheory for monads on
\mathbf{Set}
.

•

The functor
{\langle-,-\rangle}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow[\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{Set}]
maps
A,B\in\mathcal{C}

and
X\in\mathbf{Set}
to the set

{\langle A,B\rangle}(X)=\mathcal{C}(A^{X},B),

where
A^{X}
is the
X
-th power of
A
.

•

The natural transformation
(j_{C}\colon{\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{Set}}}\longrightarrow{\langle C,C\rangle})_{C\in\mathcal{C}}
corresponds by the Yoneda lemma
(note that
{\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{Set}}}\cong\mathbf{Set}(1,-)
, where
1
is a singleton)
to the family

(\overline{j}_{C}={\mathrm{id}_{C}}\in{\langle C,C\rangle}(1))_{C\in\mathcal{C}}.

•

The natural transformation
(M_{A,B,C}\colon{\langle B,C\rangle}\circ{\langle A,B\rangle}\longrightarrow{\langle A,C\rangle})_{A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}}

has the
X
-th component (
X\in\mathbf{Set}
)

{\langle B,C\rangle}\circ{\langle A,B\rangle}(X)=\mathcal{C}(B^{\mathcal{C}(A^{X},B)},C)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(A^{X},C)={\langle A,C\rangle}(X)

the function induced from the canonical morphism
A^{X}\longrightarrow B^{\mathcal{C}(A^{X},B)}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

This enrichment gives us a definition of model of a monad
\mathsf{T}

on
\mathbf{Set}
in
\mathcal{C}
.
To spell this out, first note that
for any object
C\in\mathcal{C}
, the functor
{\langle C,C\rangle}\colon\mathbf{Set}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}
which maps
X\in\mathbf{Set}
to
\mathcal{C}(C^{X},C)

acquires the monad structure, giving rise to the monad
\mathsf{End}_{{\langle-,-\rangle}}(C)

on
\mathbf{Set}
.
A model of
\mathsf{T}
is then an object
C\in\mathcal{C}
together with
a monad morphism
\mathsf{T}\longrightarrow\mathsf{End}_{{\langle-,-\rangle}}(C)
.
This is the definition of
relative algebra
of a monad on
\mathbf{Set}
by
Hino, Kobayashi, Hasuo and Jacobs
[
HKHJ16
]
.
As noted in
[
HKHJ16
]
, in the case where
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{Set}
,
relative algebras of a monad
\mathsf{T}
on
\mathbf{Set}
agree with Eilenberg–Moore
algebras of
\mathsf{T}
; we shall later show this fact
in Example
51
.

Example 41
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a large category and consider the metatheory

[\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}]
of monads on
\mathcal{S}
.
Then an enrichment over
[\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}]
is the same thing as
an
\mathcal{S}
-parameterised monad
(without strength) in the sense of
Atkey
[
Atk09
, Definition 1]
, introduced
in the study of computational effects.

Having reformulated semantics of notions of algebraic theory
in terms of enrichments, let us
investigate some of its immediate consequences.

5.1
{\mathbf{Mod}(-,-)}
as a 2-functor

It is well-known that given clones
\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{T^{\prime}}
,
a clone homomorphism
f\colon\mathsf{T}\longrightarrow\mathsf{T^{\prime}}
,
and a locally small category
\mathcal{C}
with finite products,
we have the induced functor

{\mathbf{Mod}(f,\mathcal{C})}\colon{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T^{\prime}},\mathcal{C})}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}

between the categories of models.
An equally widely known phenomenon is that
given a clone
\mathsf{T}
, locally small
categories
\mathcal{C}
and

\mathcal{C^{\prime}}
with finite products, and a functor
G\colon\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C^{\prime}}
preserving finite products, we obtain a functor

{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},G)}\colon{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C^{\prime}})}.

In order to capture such functoriality of
{\mathbf{Mod}(-,-)}
,
we introduce a 2-category of enrichments.

Definition 42

(Cf.
[
Cam18
, Definitions 2.9 and 2.10]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
be a metatheory.
The (locally large) 2-category
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}
of enrichments over
\mathcal{M}

is defined as follows:

•

An object is an enrichment
(\mathcal{C},{\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
over
\mathcal{M}
.

•

:
