Example 43
.

Let
{\mathscr{F\!P}\!ow}
be the 2-category of locally small categories with chosen finite
powers,
functors preserving finite powers (in the usual sense
4
4

4

That is,
we do not require these functors to preserve the chosen finite powers on the
nose.
)
and all natural transformations.
We have a canonical 2-functor

{\mathscr{F\!P}\!ow}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{E}\!nrich([\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}])}

which is fully faithful.

Let
{\mathscr{F\!P}\!rod}
be the 2-category of locally small categories with chosen finite
products,
functors preserving finite products (in the usual sense)
and all natural transformations.
We have a canonical 2-functor

{\mathscr{F\!P}\!rod}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{E}\!nrich([\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}])}

which is locally fully faithful.

Hence we may recover the classical functoriality of
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},-)}

for a clone
\mathsf{T}
, recalled above, if we could show that it is
functorial with respect to morphisms in
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich([\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}])}
.

We also have canonical (locally faithful) 2-functors

\displaystyle{\mathscr{S}\!ym\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}^{\mathrm{ls}}_{\mathrm{lax}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathscr{E}\!nrich([\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}])},

\displaystyle{\mathscr{S}\!ym\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}^{\mathrm{ls}}_{\mathrm{strong}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathscr{E}\!nrich({\mathscr{S}\!ym\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}(\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}))},

where the domain is the 2-category of locally small symmetric monoidal
categories, symmetric lax (resp. strong) monoidal functors and monoidal
natural transformations, and

\displaystyle{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}^{\mathrm{ls}}_{\mathrm{lax}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathscr{E}\!nrich([\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}])},

\displaystyle{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}^{\mathrm{ls}}_{\mathrm{strong}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathscr{E}\!nrich({\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}(\mathbf{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}))},

where the domain is the 2-category of locally small monoidal categories,
lax (resp. strong) monoidal functors and monoidal natural transformations.

Now the functoriality of
{\mathbf{Mod}(-,-)}

may be expressed by saying that it is a 2-functor

{\mathbf{Mod}(-,-)}\colon{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C\!A\!T}

(8)

(when we say that (
8
) is a
2-functor, we are identifying the category
{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}
with the
corresponding locally discrete 2-category).
Actually, the 2-functor (
8
) arises
immediately from the structure of

{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}
.
Observe that we may identify a theory in
\mathcal{M}
with
an enrichment of the terminal category
1
over
\mathcal{M}
.
The full sub-2-category of
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}
consisting of
all enrichments over the (fixed) terminal category
1
is in fact
locally discrete, and is isomorphic to
{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}
.
This way we obtain a fully faithful inclusion 2-functor

{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}
.
It is straightforward to see that the appropriate 2-functor
(
8
) is given by the composite

{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}
\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
,
inclusion
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}(-,-)

where
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}(-,-)
is the hom-2-functor for
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}
.

5.2
Comparing different notions of algebraic theory

So far we have been working within a fixed notion of algebraic theory.
We now turn to the question of comparing different notions of algebraic
theory.

By way of illustration,
let us consider the relationship of
clones, symmetric operads and non-symmetric operads.
On the “syntactical” side, we have inclusions
of algebraic theories

\{\text{non-sym.~operads}\}\subseteq\{\text{sym.~operads}\}\subseteq\{\text{clones}\},

(9)

in the sense that every symmetric operad may be derived from
a regular presentation of an equational theory,
which is at the same time a presentation of an equational theory
and therefore defines a clone, etc.
On the “semantical” side, in contrast, we have
inclusions of (standard) notions of models in the opposite direction,
namely:

\{\text{mon.~cat.}\}\supseteq\{\text{sym.~mon.~cat.}\}\supseteq\{\text{cat.~with fin.~prod.}\}.

(10)

Furthermore,
suppose we take the theory
\mathsf{T}
of monoids
(which is expressible as a non-symmetric operad)
and the category
\mathbf{Set}
(which has finite products).
Then we can consider the category of models
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathbf{Set})}

in three different ways:
either thinking of
\mathsf{T}
as a clone and
\mathbf{Set}
as a category with finite
products,

\mathsf{T}
as a symmetric operad and
\mathbf{Set}
as a symmetric monoidal category
(via finite products),
or
\mathsf{T}
as a non-symmetric operad and
\mathbf{Set}
as a monoidal category
(via finite products).
It turns out that the resulting three categories of models are isomorphic to
each other,
indicating certain compatibility between the three notions of algebraic theory.

The key to understand these phenomena in our framework is
the functoriality of the
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(-)}
construction.
That is, we may extend (just like base change of
enriched categories)
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(-)}
to a 2-functor

{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(-)}\colon{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}\longrightarrow\underline{2\mathchar 45\relax\mathscr{C\!A\!T}}

(11)

from the 2-category
{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}

of large monoidal categories (= metatheories), lax monoidal functors
and monoidal natural transformations,
to the 2-category
\underline{2\mathchar 45\relax\mathscr{C\!A\!T}}
of huge 2-categories, 2-functors and
2-natural transformations.
We just describe the action of a lax monoidal functor on an enrichment,
as the rest of the data for the 2-functor (
11
)
follows routinely.

Definition 44
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I_{\mathcal{M}},\otimes_{\mathcal{M}})
and

\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{N},I_{\mathcal{N}},\otimes_{\mathcal{N}})
be metatheories,

F=(F,f_{\cdot},f)\colon\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}
be a lax monoidal
functor,
5
5

5

Consisting of a functor

F\colon\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}
,
a morphism
f_{\cdot}\colon I_{\mathcal{N}}\longrightarrow FI_{\mathcal{M}}

and a natural transformation
(f_{X,Y}\colon FY\otimes_{\mathcal{N}}FX\longrightarrow F(Y\otimes_{\mathcal{M}}X))_{X,Y\in\mathcal{M}}
satisfying the suitable axioms.

\mathcal{C}
be a large category and
{\langle-,-\rangle}=({\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
be an
enrichment of
\mathcal{C}
over
\mathcal{M}
.
We define the enrichment
F_{\ast}({\langle-,-\rangle})=({\langle-,-\rangle}^{\prime},j^{\prime},M^{\prime})

of
\mathcal{C}
over
\mathcal{N}
as follows:

•

The functor

{\langle-,-\rangle}^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}

maps
(A,B)\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}
to
F{\langle A,B\rangle}
.

•

The natural transformation
(j^{\prime}_{C}\colon I_{\mathcal{N}}\longrightarrow{\langle C,C\rangle}^{\prime})_{C\in\mathcal{C}}

is defined by
j^{\prime}_{C}=Fj_{C}\circ f_{\cdot}
:

I_{\mathcal{N}}
FI_{\mathcal{M}}
F{\langle C,C\rangle}
.
f_{\cdot}
Fj_{C}

•

The natural transformation
(M^{\prime}_{A,B,C}\colon{\langle B,C\rangle}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{N}}{\langle A,B\rangle}^{\prime}\longrightarrow{\langle A,C\rangle}^{\prime})_{A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}}

is defined by
M^{\prime}_{A,B,C}=FM_{A,B,C}\circ f_{{\langle A,B\rangle},{\langle B,C\rangle}}
:

F{\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes_{\mathcal{N}}F{\langle A,B\rangle}
F({\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes_{\mathcal{M}}{\langle A,B\rangle})
F{\langle A,C\rangle}
.
f_{{\langle A,B\rangle},{\langle B,C\rangle}}
FM_{A,B,C}

As an immediate consequence of the 2-functoriality
of
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(-)}
,
it follows that whenever we have a monoidal adjunction (adjunction in

{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}
)

\mathcal{M}
\mathcal{N}
,
L
R
\dashv

we obtain a 2-adjunction

{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{N})}
.
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(L)}
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(R)}
\dashv

Therefore, if we take

\mathsf{T}\in{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}\subseteq{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}

and
(\mathcal{C},{\langle-,-\rangle})\in{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{N})}
in this situation,
then we get an isomorphism of categories

{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}(\mathsf{T},{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(R)}(\mathcal{C},{\langle-,-\rangle}))\cong{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{N})}({\mathscr{E}\!nrich(L)}(\mathsf{T}),(\mathcal{C},{\langle-,-\rangle})).

(12)

Since the action of
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(-)}
preserves the underlying categories,
we may regard
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(L)}(\mathsf{T})
as a theory in
\mathcal{N}
.
Therefore
(
12
) may be seen as an isomorphism between
the category of models of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
with
respect to
R_{\ast}({\langle-,-\rangle})
,
and the category of models of
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(L)}(\mathsf{T})

in
\mathcal{C}
with respect to
{\langle-,-\rangle}
.

The relationship between clones, symmetric operads and non-symmetric operads
mentioned above can be explained in this way.
First note that there is a chain of inclusions

\mathbf{N}
\mathbf{P}
\mathbf{F}
.
J
J^{\prime}

Therefore, precomposition and left Kan extensions
induce a chain of adjunctions

[\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}]
[\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}]
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
.
\mathrm{Lan}_{J}
[J,\mathbf{Set}]
\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime}}
[J^{\prime},\mathbf{Set}]
\dashv
\dashv

It turns out that these adjunctions acquire natural structures of
monoidal adjunctions
(with respect to the substitution monoidal structures).
Hence in our framework, the inclusions
(
9
) are expressed as the functors

{\mathbf{Th}([\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}])}
{\mathbf{Th}([\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}])}
{\mathbf{Th}([\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}])}
{\mathbf{Th}(\mathrm{Lan}_{J})}
{\mathbf{Th}(\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime}})}

between the categories of theories,
whereas the inclusions (
10
) are restrictions of
the 2-functors

{\mathscr{E}\!nrich([\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}])}
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich([\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}])}
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich([\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}])}
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich([J,\mathbf{Set}])}
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich([{J^{\prime}},\mathbf{Set}])}

between the 2-categories of enrichments.

6
Notions of model as oplax actions

In order to capture the standard notions of model for monads and generalised
operads,
enrichments do not suffice in general.
A suitable structure is
oplax action
, defined as follows.

Definition 45
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
be a metatheory.
An
oplax action of
\mathcal{M}
consists of:

•

a large category
\mathcal{C}
;

•

a functor
\ast\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
;

•

a natural transformation
(\varepsilon_{C}\colon I\ast C\longrightarrow C)_{C\in\mathcal{C}}
;

•

a natural transformation

(\delta_{X,Y,C}\colon(Y\otimes X)\ast C\longrightarrow Y\ast(X\ast C))_{X,Y\in\mathcal{M},C\in\mathcal{C}},

We have chosen to set

,
because the former agrees with
the convention to write composition of morphisms in the
anti-diagrammatic order, which we adopt in
this paper.

making the following diagrams commute for all

We say that
(\mathcal{C},\ast,\varepsilon,\delta)
is
an oplax action of
\mathcal{M}
, or that

.

An oplax action
(\ast,\varepsilon,\delta)
of
\mathcal{M}

on
\mathcal{C}
is
called a

(resp.

)
if both
\varepsilon
and
\delta
are natural isomorphisms
(resp. identities).

The definition of model we derive from an oplax action is
the following (cf.
[

, Section 2.2]
).

.

\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)

.

Example 47
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a large category.
Recall that the metatheory for monads on
\mathcal{C}
is

[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]
.
We have a strict action

\ast\colon[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

given by evaluation:
(X,C)\longmapsto XC
.

This clearly generates the definitions of
Eilenberg–Moore algebra and

14
).

Example 48
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a large category with finite limits and

\mathsf{S}=(S,\eta,\mu)

be a cartesian monad on
\mathcal{C}
.
Recall that the metatheory for
\mathsf{S}
-operads is

\mathcal{C}/S1
.
Models of an
\mathsf{S}
-operad and their homomorphisms
(Definition
19
) were
introduced by using the pseudo action

\ast\colon(\mathcal{C}/S1)\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

in the first place, and therefore are an instance of the above
general definitions.

6.1
The 2-category of oplax actions of
\mathcal{M}

For a metatheory
\mathcal{M}
,
we can define the 2-category of oplax actions of
\mathcal{M}

(cf. Definition
42
).

Definition 49
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
be a metatheory.
The (locally large) 2-category
{\mathscr{A}\!ct_{\mathrm{oplax}}(\mathcal{M})}
of oplax actions of
\mathcal{M}

is defined as follows:

•

An object is an oplax action
(\mathcal{C},\ast,\varepsilon,\delta)
of

\mathcal{M}
.

•

A 1-cell from
(\mathcal{C},\ast,\varepsilon,\delta)
to

(\mathcal{C^{\prime}},\ast^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
is a functor
G\colon\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C^{\prime}}
together with a natural transformation

(g_{X,C}\colon X\ast^{\prime}GC\longrightarrow G(X\ast C))_{X\in\mathcal{M},C\in\mathcal{C}}

making the following diagrams commute for all
X,Y\in\mathcal{M}
and

:

I\ast^{\prime}GC

Similarly to the case of enrichments, we may
extend the
{\mathbf{Mod}(-,-)}
construction into a 2-functor

{\mathbf{Mod}(-,-)}\colon{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathscr{A}\!ct_{\mathrm{oplax}}(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C\!A\!T}.

On the other hand,
{\mathscr{A}\!ct_{\mathrm{oplax}}(-)}
extends to a 2-functor
in an apparently different manner than
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(-)}
.
Namely, it is a 2-functor of type

{\mathscr{A}\!ct_{\mathrm{oplax}}(-)}\colon({\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{oplax}}})^{\mathrm{coop}}\longrightarrow\underline{2\mathchar 45\relax\mathscr{C\!A\!T}},

where
{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{oplax}}}
is the 2-category of large monoidal categories
(= metatheories),
oplax monoidal functors and monoidal natural transformations.
The apparent discrepancy between functoriality of
{\mathscr{A}\!ct_{\mathrm{oplax}}(-)}

and
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(-)}

will be solved in Section
8

with the introduction of morphisms of metatheories.

6.2
The relation between enrichments and oplax actions

We have introduced two types of structures—enrichment and oplax action—to
formalise notions of model.
The former captures the standard notions of model
for clones, symmetric operads, non-symmetric operads, PROPs and PROs,
whereas the latter captures those for
monads and generalised operads.
We will unify enrichment and oplax action by
the notion of
metamodel
in the next section,
but before doing so we remark on the relationship
between them.
We also explain why in some good cases we can give definition of model
both in terms of enrichment and oplax actions;
for instances of this phenomenon in the literature,
see e.g.,
[
Kel74a
, Section 3]
and

[
Lei04
, Section 6.4]
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
be a metatheory and

\mathcal{C}
be a large category.
The relationship between enrichment and oplax action is summarised in
the adjunction

\mathcal{M}
\mathcal{C}
.
-\ast C
{\langle C,-\rangle}
\dashv

(13)

In more detail, what we mean is the following.
Suppose that we have an enrichment
({\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)

of
\mathcal{C}
over
\mathcal{M}
.
If, in addition, for each
C\in\mathcal{C}
the functor
{\langle C,-\rangle}

has a left adjoint as in (
13
),
then—by the parameter theorem
for adjunctions; see
[
ML98
, Section IV.7]
—the left adjoints
canonically extend to a
bifunctor
\ast\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
,
and
j
and
M
define appropriate natural transformations
\varepsilon

and
\delta
, giving rise to an oplax action
(\ast,\varepsilon,\delta)

of
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{C}
.
And vice versa, if we start from an oplax action.

To make this idea into a precise mathematical statement, let us introduce the
following 2-categories.

Definition 50
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a metatheory.

1.

Let
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich^{\mathrm{r}}(\mathcal{M})}
be the full sub-2-category of
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}

consisting of all enrichments
(\mathcal{C},{\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
such that
for each
C\in\mathcal{C}
,
{\langle C,-\rangle}
is a right adjoint.

2.

Let
{\mathscr{A}\!ct^{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{oplax}}(\mathcal{M})}
be the full sub-2-category of
{\mathscr{A}\!ct_{\mathrm{oplax}}(\mathcal{M})}

consisting of all oplax actions
(\mathcal{C},\ast,\varepsilon,\delta)

such that for each
C\in\mathcal{C}
,
-\ast C
is a left adjoint.

The above discussion can be summarised in the
statement that the two 2-categories
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich^{\mathrm{r}}(\mathcal{M})}
and
{\mathscr{A}\!ct^{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{oplax}}(\mathcal{M})}

are equivalent.
A direct proof of this equivalence would be essentially routine,
but seems to involve rather lengthy calculation.
We shall defer a proof to Corollary
61
.

This observation, also mentioned in
[
Cam18
]
in a more general setting, is a variant of well-known categorical folklore.
In the literature, it is usually stated in a slightly more restricted form than
the above, for example as a correspondence between tensored
\mathcal{M}
-categories
and closed pseudo actions of

\mathcal{M}

[
Kel74a
,
GP97
,
Lin81
,
JK01
]
.

Furthermore, the above correspondence is
compatible with the definitions of model (Definitions
34

and
46
).
Suppose that
(\mathcal{C},{\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
and

(\mathcal{C},\ast,\varepsilon,\delta)

form a pair of an enrichment over
\mathcal{M}
and an oplax action of
\mathcal{M}

connected by the adjunctions (
13
)
(in a way compatible with the natural transformations

j,M,\varepsilon
and
\delta
).
Then for any theory
\mathsf{T}=(T,e,m)
in
\mathcal{M}
and any
object
C\in\mathcal{C}
,
a morphism

\chi\colon T\longrightarrow{\langle C,C\rangle}

is a model of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
with respect to
{\langle-,-\rangle}

(Definition
34
)
if and only if its transpose under the adjunction
-\ast C\dashv{\langle C,-\rangle}

\gamma\colon T\ast C\longrightarrow C

is a model of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
with respect to
\ast

(Definition
46
),
and similarly for homomorphism between models of
\mathsf{T}
.
Hence we obtain an isomorphism of categories

{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},{\langle-,-\rangle}))}\cong{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\ast))}

commuting with the forgetful functors into
\mathcal{C}
.

Some of the enrichments and oplax actions we have introduced so far
are good enough to obtain the corresponding oplax actions or enrichments,
giving rise to alternative definitions of model.

Example 51
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small category with all small powers.
Recall the strict action

\ast\colon[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

of the metatheory
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]
for monads
on
\mathcal{C}
, used to capture their Eilenberg–Moore algebras.
For any object
C\in\mathcal{C}
, write by
{\lceil C\rceil}\colon 1\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

the functor from the terminal category
1
which maps the unique object of
1

to
C\in\mathcal{C}
(the
name of
C
).

By the assumptions on
\mathcal{C}
,
for any object
A\in\mathcal{C}
the functor
-\ast A
(which may be seen as
the precomposition by
{\lceil A\rceil}\colon 1\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
) admits
a right adjoint
{\langle A,-\rangle}
, which maps any
B\in\mathcal{C}
(equivalently,

{\lceil B\rceil}\colon 1\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
) to the right Kan extension

{\langle A,B\rangle}=\mathrm{Ran}_{{\lceil A\rceil}}{\lceil B\rceil}
of
{\lceil B\rceil}
along
{\lceil A\rceil}
.
The functor
\mathrm{Ran}_{{\lceil A\rceil}}{\lceil B\rceil}\colon\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

maps
C\in\mathcal{C}
to
\mathrm{Ran}_{{\lceil A\rceil}}{\lceil B\rceil}(C)=B^{\mathcal{C}(C,A)}
.

For any object
C\in\mathcal{C}
,
\mathrm{Ran}_{{\lceil C\rceil}}{\lceil C\rceil}

exists and acquires a canonical monad structure (the
codensity
monad of
{\lceil C\rceil}
).
For any monad
\mathsf{T}
on
\mathcal{C}
, to give a structure of an
Eilenberg–Moore algebra on
C\in\mathcal{C}
is equivalent to
give a monad morphism from
\mathsf{T}
to
\mathrm{Ran}_{{\lceil C\rceil}}{\lceil C\rceil}
.
This observation is in e.g.,
[
Kel74a
, Section 3]
.

In particular, if we take
\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{Set}
,
we see that the above enrichment agrees with the one given
in Example
40
.
Hence the notion of relative algebra
[
HKHJ16
]
agrees with
that of Eilenberg–Moore algebra in this case.

7
Metamodels and models

In Sections
5
and
6
,
we have seen that the standard notions of model for various notions of
algebraic theory can be formalised either as enrichments or
as oplax actions.
With
two
definitions, however, we cannot claim to have
formalised notions of model in a sufficiently satisfactory manner.
We now unify enrichments and oplax actions by introducing
a more general structure of

metamodel
(of a metatheory).
We also derive definitions of models of theories and of their homomorphisms
from a metamodel, and show that they generalise the corresponding
definitions for enrichments and oplax actions.

We may approach
the concept of metamodel of a metatheory
\mathcal{M}

in two different ways, one by generalising
enrichments over
\mathcal{M}
and the other
by generalising oplax actions of
\mathcal{M}
.
Before giving a formal (and neutral) definition of metamodel, we describe
these two perspectives.

7.1
Metamodels as generalised enrichments

Let us first discuss how a generalisation of enrichments over
\mathcal{M}

leads to the notion of metamodel.
For this, we use the construction known as the

Day convolution

[
Day70
]
.
Given any metatheory
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
,
this construction endows the presheaf category

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=[\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{SET}]

with a (biclosed) monoidal structure
(\widehat{I},\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}})
,
in such a way that the Yoneda embedding

\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
canonically becomes strong monoidal.

Definition 52

(
[
Day70
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
be a metatheory.
The
convolution monoidal structure

(\widehat{I},\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}})
on the presheaf category
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=[\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{SET}]
is defined as follows.

•

The unit object
\widehat{I}
is the representable functor

\mathcal{M}(-,I)\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
.

•

Given
P,Q\in\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
, their monoidal product

Q\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}P\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
maps
Z\in\mathcal{M}

to

(Q\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}P)(Z)=\int^{X,Y\in\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}(Z,Y\otimes X)\times Q(Y)\times P(X).

For a metatheory
\mathcal{M}
,
a
metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
is simply an enrichment over

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=(\widehat{\mathcal{M}},\widehat{I},\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}})
.
7
7

7

Although
we have defined enrichment (Definition
33
)
only for metatheories, i.e., large monoidal categories,
the definition does not depend on any size condition
and it is clear what we mean by enrichments over non-large monoidal categories,
such as
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
.

Thanks to the strong monoidal Yoneda embedding,
every enrichment over
\mathcal{M}
induces a metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
.

We can find several uses of
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
-categories
(in the sense of enriched category theory) in the literature.
In particular,
[
KLSS02
, Section 6]
and

[
Mel12
]
contain
discussions on relationship between
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
-categories
and various actions of
\mathcal{M}
.

7.2
Metamodels as generalised oplax actions

Let us move on to the second perspective on metamodels,
namely as generalised oplax actions.
First note that an oplax action
(\mathcal{C},\ast,\varepsilon,\delta)

of a metatheory
\mathcal{M}

can be equivalently given as an oplax monoidal functor

\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]

defined by
X\longmapsto X\ast-
,
or as a colax functor

\Sigma\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathscr{C\!A\!T},

(14)

where
\Sigma\mathcal{M}
denotes
\mathcal{M}
seen as a one-object
bicategory
[
Bén67
]
.

To generalise this,
we use the bicategory
{\mathscr{P\!R\!O\!F}}
of profunctors
(Definition
31
).
It is well-known that
both
\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
and
\mathscr{C\!A\!T}^{\mathrm{coop}}
canonically
embed into
{\mathscr{P\!R\!O\!F}}
.
Both embeddings are identity-on-objects and locally fully faithful
pseudofunctors.
The embedding

(-)_{\ast}\colon\mathscr{C\!A\!T}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{P\!R\!O\!F}}

maps a functor
F\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}

to the profunctor
F_{\ast}\colon\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{B}
defined by

F_{\ast}(B,A)=\mathcal{B}(B,FA)
, whereas
the embedding

(-)^{\ast}\colon\mathscr{C\!A\!T}^{\mathrm{coop}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{P\!R\!O\!F}}

maps a functor
F\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}
to
the profunctor
F^{\ast}\colon\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{A}

with
F^{\ast}(A,B)=\mathcal{B}(FA,B)
.
Moreover, for any functor
F\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}

we have an adjunction
F_{\ast}\dashv F^{\ast}
in
{\mathscr{P\!R\!O\!F}}
.

A
metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
is a colax functor

\Sigma\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{P\!R\!O\!F}}^{\mathrm{coop}},

or equivalently a lax functor

(\Sigma\mathcal{M})^{\mathrm{co}}=\Sigma(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}})\longrightarrow{\mathscr{P\!R\!O\!F}}^{\mathrm{op}}.

(15)

Clearly, oplax actions of
\mathcal{M}
, in the form
(
14
),
give rise to metamodels of
\mathcal{M}
by postcomposing the pseudofunctor

(-)^{\ast}
.

Let us restate what a lax functor of type (
15
)
amounts to, in monoidal categorical terms.

Definition 53
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a large category.
Define the monoidal category
[\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbf{SET}]=([\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbf{SET}],\mathcal{C}(-,-),\mathbin{\odot^{\mathrm{rev}}})
of

endo-profunctors on
\mathcal{C}

to be the endo-hom-category
{\mathscr{P\!R\!O\!F}}^{\mathrm{op}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
.
More precisely:

•

The unit object is the hom-functor

\mathcal{C}(-,-)\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
.

•

Given
H,K\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
,
their monoidal product
H\mathbin{\odot^{\mathrm{rev}}}K

maps
(A,C)\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}
to

(H\mathbin{\odot^{\mathrm{rev}}}K)(A,C)=\int^{B\in\mathcal{C}}H(B,C)\times K(A,B).

Note that
H\mathbin{\odot^{\mathrm{rev}}}K\cong K\odot H
(i.e.,
\mathbin{\odot^{\mathrm{rev}}}
is
“
\odot
reversed”).

Using this monoidal structure on
[\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbf{SET}]
, a
metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
in a large category
\mathcal{C}

may be written as a lax monoidal functor

\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow[\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbf{SET}].

We now give the neutral definition of metamodels.

Definition 54

(Cf.
[
Cam18
, Definition 4.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},\otimes,I)
be a metatheory.
A
metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
consists of:

•

a large category
\mathcal{C}
;

•

a functor

\Phi\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}

(whose action we write as
(X,A,B)\longmapsto\Phi_{X}(A,B)
);

•

a natural transformation
((\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{C}\colon 1\longrightarrow\Phi_{I}(C,C))_{C\in\mathcal{C}}
;

•

a natural transformation

((\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}\colon\Phi_{Y}(B,C)\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)\longrightarrow\Phi_{Y\otimes X}(A,C))_{X,Y\in\mathcal{M},A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}},

making the following diagrams commute
for all
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{M}
and
A,B,C,D\in\mathcal{C}
:

1\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)
\Phi_{I}(B,B)\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)
\Phi_{X}(A,B)
\Phi_{I\otimes X}(A,B)
(\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{B}\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)
(\overline{\phi}_{X,I})_{A,B,B}
\cong
\cong

\Phi_{X}(A,B)\times 1
\Phi_{X}(A,B)\times\Phi_{I}(A,A)
\Phi_{X}(A,B)
\Phi_{X\otimes I}(A,B)
\Phi_{X}(A,B)\times(\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{A}
(\overline{\phi}_{I,X})_{A,A,B}
\cong
\cong

\big(\Phi_{Z}(C,D)\times\Phi_{Y}(B,C)\big)\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)
\Phi_{Z\otimes Y}(B,D)\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)
\Phi_{(Z\otimes Y)\otimes X}(A,D)
\Phi_{Z}(C,D)\times\big(\Phi_{Y}(B,C)\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)\big)
\Phi_{Z}(C,D)\times\Phi_{Y\otimes X}(A,C)
\Phi_{Z\otimes(Y\otimes X)}(A,D).
(\overline{\phi}_{Y,Z})_{B,C,D}\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Z\otimes Y})_{A,B,D}
\cong
\cong
\Phi_{Z}(C,D)\times(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}
(\overline{\phi}_{Y\otimes X,Z})_{A,C,D}

We say that
(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
is a
metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
,
or that
(\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
is a
metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

The above definition perfectly makes sense even if we replace
the category
\mathbf{SET}
of large sets by the category
\mathbf{Set}

of small sets.
Indeed, most of the naturally occurring notions of model
can be captured by such “
\mathbf{Set}
-valued” metamodels.
However, for theoretical development it turns out to be more
convenient to define metamodels as above.

Note that
we may replace
((\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{C})_{C\in\mathcal{C}}
by

((j_{C})_{Z}\colon\widehat{I}(Z)\longrightarrow\Phi_{Z}(C,C))_{C\in\mathcal{C},Z\in\mathcal{M}}

and
((\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C})_{X,Y\in\mathcal{M},A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}}
by

((M_{A,B,C})_{Z}\colon(\Phi_{(-)}(B,C)\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}\Phi_{(-)}(A,B))(Z)\longrightarrow\Phi_{Z}(A,C))_{A,B,C\in\mathcal{M},Z\in\mathcal{M}}.

The axioms for metamodel then translate to the ones for
enrichments (over
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
).

On the other hand, we may also replace

((\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{C})_{C\in\mathcal{C}}
by

((\phi_{\cdot})_{A,B}\colon\mathcal{C}(A,B)\longrightarrow\Phi_{I}(A,B))_{A,B\in\mathcal{C}}

and
((\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C})_{X,Y\in\mathcal{M},A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}}
by

((\phi_{X,Y})_{A,C}\colon(\Phi_{Y}\mathbin{\odot^{\mathrm{rev}}}\Phi_{X})(A,C)\longrightarrow\Phi_{Y\otimes X}(A,C))_{A,C\in\mathcal{C},X,Y\in\mathcal{M}}.

The axioms for metamodel then state that

(\Phi,\phi_{\cdot},\phi)\colon(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}},I,\otimes)\longrightarrow([\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C},\mathbf{SET}],\mathcal{C}(-,-),\mathbin{\odot^{\mathrm{rev}}})

is an oplax monoidal functor.

Hence the attempts to generalise enrichments and oplax actions mentioned above
coincide and both give rise to Definition
54
.

The definitions of model and homomorphism we derive from a metamodel
are the following.

Definition 55
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
be a metatheory,

\mathsf{T}=(T,e,m)
be a theory in
\mathcal{M}
,

\mathcal{C}
be a large category and

\Phi=(\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})

be a metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

1.

A
model of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
with respect to
\Phi

is a pair
(C,\xi)
consisting of an object
C
of
\mathcal{C}

and an element
\xi\in\Phi_{T}(C,C)
such that

(\Phi_{e})_{C,C}(\xi)=(\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{C}(\ast)
(where
\ast

is the unique element of
1
)
and
(\Phi_{m})_{C,C}(\xi)=(\overline{\phi}_{T,T})_{C,C,C}(\xi,\xi)
:

\Phi_{T}(C,C)
\Phi_{I}(C,C)
1
(\Phi_{e})_{C,C}
(\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{C}

\Phi_{T}(C,C)
\Phi_{T\otimes T}(C,C).
\Phi_{T}(C,C)\times\Phi_{T}(C,C)
(\Phi_{m})_{C,C}
(\overline{\phi}_{T,T})_{C,C,C}

2.

Let
(C,\xi)
and
(C^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
be models of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}

with respect to
\Phi
. A
homomorphism from
(C,\xi)
to
(C^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})

is a morphism
f\colon C\longrightarrow C^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
such that

\Phi_{T}(C,f)(\xi)=\Phi_{T}(f,C^{\prime})(\xi^{\prime})
:

\Phi_{T}(C,C)
\Phi_{T}(C,C^{\prime}).
\Phi_{T}(C^{\prime},C^{\prime})
\Phi_{T}(C,f)
\Phi_{T}(f,C^{\prime})

We denote the (large) category of models of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
with
respect to

\Phi
by
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
.

Example 56
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
be a metatheory,

\mathcal{C}
be a large category and

({\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
be an enrichment of
\mathcal{C}
over
\mathcal{M}
.
This induces a metamodel
(\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})

of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{C}
as follows.

•

The functor

\Phi\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}

maps
(X,A,B)\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}

to

\Phi_{X}(A,B)=\mathcal{M}(X,{\langle A,B\rangle}).

•

For each
C\in\mathcal{C}
,
(\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{C}\colon 1\longrightarrow\Phi_{I}(C,C)

is the name of
j_{C}

(i.e.,
(\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{C}
maps the unique element of the singleton

1
to
{j_{C}}
).

•

For each
A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}
and
X,Y\in\mathcal{M}
, the function

(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}\colon\Phi_{Y}(B,C)\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)\longrightarrow\Phi_{Y\otimes X}(A,C)

maps
g\colon Y\longrightarrow{\langle B,C\rangle}
and
f\colon X\longrightarrow{\langle A,B\rangle}
to

Y\otimes X
{\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}
{\langle A,C\rangle}.
g\otimes f
M_{A,B,C}

The definition of model and homomorphism (Definition
34
)
we derive from an enrichment may be seen as
a special case of the
corresponding definition (Definition
55
)
for metamodel.

Example 57
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)

\Phi_{X}(A,B)=\mathcal{C}(X\ast A,{B}).

For each

is the name of
\varepsilon_{C}
.

For each

and

, the function

(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}\colon\Phi_{Y}(B,C)\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)\longrightarrow\Phi_{Y\otimes X}(A,C)

maps
g\colon Y\ast{B}\longrightarrow{C}
and
f\colon X\ast{A}\longrightarrow{B}
to

(Y\otimes X)\ast A
Y\ast(X\ast A)
Y\ast B
C.
\delta_{X,Y,A}
Y\ast f
g

The definition of model and homomorphism
(Definition
46
)
we derive from an oplax action may be seen as a special case of the
