)
for metamodel.

7.3
The 2-category of metamodels

Metamodels of a metatheory naturally form a 2-category,
just as enrichments and oplax actions do.

Definition 58

(Cf.
[
Cam18
, Definitions 4.10 and 4.11]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
be a metatheory.
We define the (locally large) 2-category
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}
of
metamodels of
\mathcal{M}
as follows.

•

An object is a metamodel

(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
of
\mathcal{M}
.

•

A 1-cell from
(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
to

(\mathcal{C^{\prime}},\Phi^{\prime},\overline{\phi^{\prime}}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi^{\prime}})

is a functor
G\colon\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C^{\prime}}

together with a natural transformation
(g_{X,A,B}\colon\Phi_{X}(A,B)\longrightarrow\Phi^{\prime}_{X}(GA,GB))_{X\in\mathcal{M},A,B\in\mathcal{C}}

making the following
diagrams commute for all

X,Y\in\mathcal{M}
and
A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}
:

1
\Phi_{I}(C,C)
\Phi^{\prime}_{I}(GC,GC)
(\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{C}
g_{I,C,C}
(\overline{\phi^{\prime}}_{\cdot})_{GC}

\Phi_{Y}(B,C)\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)
\Phi_{Y\otimes X}(A,C)
\Phi^{\prime}_{Y}(GB,GC)\times\Phi^{\prime}_{X}(GA,GB)
\Phi^{\prime}_{Y\otimes X}(GA,GC).
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}
g_{Y\otimes X,A,C}
g_{Y,B,C}\times g_{X,A,B}
(\overline{\phi^{\prime}}_{X,Y})_{GA,GB,GC}

•

A 2-cell from
(G,g)
to
(G^{\prime},g^{\prime})
, both from

(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
to

(\mathcal{C^{\prime}},\Phi^{\prime},\overline{\phi^{\prime}}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi^{\prime}})
,
is a natural transformation
\theta\colon G\Longrightarrow G^{\prime}

making the following diagram commute for all
X\in\mathcal{M}

and
A,B\in\mathcal{C}
:

\Phi_{X}(A,B)
\Phi^{\prime}_{X}(GA,GB)
\Phi^{\prime}_{X}(G^{\prime}A,G^{\prime}B)
\Phi^{\prime}_{X}(GA,G^{\prime}B).
g_{X,A,B}
\Phi^{\prime}_{X}(GA,\theta_{B})
g^{\prime}_{X,A,B}
\Phi^{\prime}_{X}(\theta_{A},G^{\prime}B)

Recall that for a functor (resp. a 2-functor)

F\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}
,
the
essential image of
F
is the full subcategory
(resp. full sub-2-category) of
\mathcal{B}

consisting of all objects
B\in\mathcal{B}
such that there exists an object

A\in\mathcal{A}
and an isomorphism
FA\cong B
.
If
\mathcal{A}
is a large category, a contravariant presheaf

\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}

(resp. a covariant presheaf
\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
)
over
\mathcal{A}
is
called
representable
if and only if it is
in the essential image of the Yoneda embedding

\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow[\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{SET}]

(resp.
\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow[\mathcal{A},\mathbf{SET}]^{\mathrm{op}}
).

Proposition 59

(Cf.
[
Cam18
, Proposition 4.12 (b)]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a metatheory.
The construction given in Example
56

canonically extends to a fully faithful 2-functor

{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}.

A metamodel
(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})

of
\mathcal{M}
is in the essential image of this 2-functor if and only if
for each
A,B\in\mathcal{C}
, the functor

\Phi_{(-)}(A,B)\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}

is representable.

Proof.

The construction of the
2-functor
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}

is straightforward.
The rest can also be proved by a standard argument using the Yoneda lemma.
We sketch the argument below.

Let us focus on the characterisation of the essential image.
Suppose that
(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
is a
metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
such that
for each
A,B\in\mathcal{C}
, the functor
\Phi_{(-)}(A,B)

is representable.
From such a metamodel we obtain an enrichment
({\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
of

\mathcal{C}
over
\mathcal{M}
as follows.
For each
A,B\in\mathcal{C}
, choose an object
{\langle A,B\rangle}\in\mathcal{M}
and an
isomorphism

\alpha_{A,B}\colon\mathcal{M}(-,{\langle A,B\rangle})\longrightarrow\Phi_{(-)}(A,B)
.
By functoriality of
\Phi
,
{\langle-,-\rangle}
uniquely extends to a
functor of type

\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}
while making

(\alpha_{A,B})_{A,B\in\mathcal{C}}
natural.
For each
C\in\mathcal{C}
,
(\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{C}\colon 1\longrightarrow\Phi_{I}(C,C)\cong\mathcal{M}(I,{\langle C,C\rangle})
gives rise to a
morphism
j_{C}\colon I\longrightarrow{\langle C,C\rangle}
in
\mathcal{M}
.
For each
A,B,C\in\mathcal{M}
, consider the function

\mathcal{M}({\langle B,C\rangle},{\langle B,C\rangle})\times\mathcal{M}({\langle A,B\rangle},{\langle A,B\rangle})
\Phi_{{\langle B,C\rangle}}(B,C)\times\Phi_{\langle A,B\rangle}(A,B)
\Phi_{{\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}}(A,C)
\mathcal{M}({\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle},{\langle A,C\rangle}).
(\overline{\phi}_{{\langle A,B\rangle},{\langle B,C\rangle}})_{A,B,C}
(\alpha_{B,C})_{\langle B,C\rangle}\times(\alpha_{A,B})_{{\langle A,B\rangle}}
(\alpha_{A,C})_{{\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}}^{-1}

Let the image of
({\mathrm{id}_{{\langle B,C\rangle}}},{\mathrm{id}_{{\langle A,B\rangle}}})

under this function be
M_{A,B,C}\colon{\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}\longrightarrow{\langle A,C\rangle}
.
The axioms of metamodel then shows that
({\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
is an enrichment.

Moreover, if we consider the metamodel induced from this enrichment
(see Example
56
), then it is
isomorphic to our original

(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
.
In particular, for each
X,Y\in\mathcal{M}
and
A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}
,
the function
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}
is completely
determined by
M_{A,B,C}
, as in
Example
56
.
To see this, note that for each
f\in\mathcal{M}(X,{\langle A,B\rangle})

and
g\in\mathcal{M}(Y,{\langle B,C\rangle})
, the diagram

\mathcal{M}(Y,{\langle B,C\rangle})\times\mathcal{M}(X,{\langle A,B\rangle})
\Phi_{Y}(B,C)\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)
\Phi_{Y\otimes X}(A,C)
\mathcal{M}({\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle},{\langle A,C\rangle})
\mathcal{M}({\langle B,C\rangle},{\langle B,C\rangle})\times\mathcal{M}({\langle A,B\rangle},{\langle A,B\rangle})
\Phi_{{\langle B,C\rangle}}(B,C)\times\Phi_{\langle A,B\rangle}(A,B)
\Phi_{{\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle}}(A,C)
\mathcal{M}({\langle B,C\rangle}\otimes{\langle A,B\rangle},{\langle A,C\rangle})
(\overline{\phi}_{{\langle A,B\rangle},{\langle B,C\rangle}})_{A,B,C}
\alpha\times\alpha
\alpha^{-1}
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}
\alpha\times\alpha
\alpha^{-1}
\mathcal{M}(g,{\mathrm{id}})\times\mathcal{M}(f,{\mathrm{id}})
\Phi_{g}(B,C)\times\Phi_{f}(A,B)
\Phi_{g\otimes f}(A,C)
\mathcal{M}(g\otimes f,{\mathrm{id}})

commutes. Hence by chasing the element
({\mathrm{id}_{{\langle B,C\rangle}}},{\mathrm{id}_{{\langle A,B\rangle}}})

in the top left set, we observe that (modulo the isomorphisms
\alpha
)

(g,f)
is mapped by
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}
to

M_{A,B,C}\circ(g\otimes f)
.
∎

Proposition 60

(Cf.
[
Cam18
, Proposition 4.2 (a)]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a metatheory.
The construction given in Example
57

canonically extends to a fully faithful 2-functor

{\mathscr{A}\!ct_{\mathrm{oplax}}(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}.

A metamodel
(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
of
\mathcal{M}

is in the essential image of this 2-functor if and only if for each

X\in\mathcal{M}
and
A\in\mathcal{C}
, the functor

\Phi_{X}(A,-)\colon\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}

is representable.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of Proposition
59
.

In particular, given a metamodel

(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
of
\mathcal{M}
such that
for each
X\in\mathcal{M}
and
A\in\mathcal{C}
, the functor
\Phi_{X}(A,-)
is
representable, we may construct an oplax action
(\ast,\varepsilon,\delta)
of

\mathcal{C}
as follows.
For each
X\in\mathcal{M}
and
A\in\mathcal{C}
, choose an object
X\ast A\in\mathcal{C}

and an isomorphism
\beta_{X,A}\colon\mathcal{C}(X\ast A,-)\longrightarrow\Phi_{X}(A,-)
.
We easily obtain a functor

\ast\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

and a natural transformation
(\varepsilon_{C})_{C\in\mathcal{C}}
.
To get
\delta
, for each
X,Y\in\mathcal{M}
and
A\in\mathcal{C}
consider
the function

\mathcal{C}(Y\ast(X\ast A),Y\ast(X\ast A))\times\mathcal{C}(X\ast A,X\ast A)
\Phi_{Y}(X\ast A,Y\ast(X\ast A))\times\Phi_{X}(A,X\ast A)
\Phi_{Y\otimes X}(A,Y\ast(X\ast A))
\mathcal{C}((Y\otimes X)\ast A,Y\ast(X\ast A)).
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,X\ast A,Y\ast(X\ast A)}
(\beta_{Y,X\ast A})_{Y\ast(X\ast A)}\times(\beta_{X,A})_{X\ast A}
(\beta_{Y\otimes X,A})_{Y\ast(X\ast A)}^{-1}

We define
\delta_{X,Y,A}\colon(Y\otimes X)\ast A\longrightarrow Y\ast(X\ast A)
to be the image of
({\mathrm{id}_{Y\ast(X\ast A)}},{\mathrm{id}_{X\ast A}})
under this
function.

To verify that the metamodel induced from this oplax action (see
Example
57
) is isomorphic to

(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
,
essentially we only need to check that
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}

for each
X,Y\in\mathcal{M}
and
A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}
is determined by

\delta_{X,Y,A}
as in Example
57
.
Suppressing the isomorphisms
\beta
from now on,
for each
f\colon X\ast A\longrightarrow B
and
g\colon Y\ast B\longrightarrow C
consider the following digram:

\mathcal{C}((Y\otimes X)\ast A,Y\ast(X\ast A))
\mathcal{C}((Y\otimes X)\ast A,C)
\mathcal{C}(Y\ast B,C)\times\mathcal{C}(X\ast A,B).
\mathcal{C}(Y\ast(X\ast A),Y\ast(X\ast A))\times\mathcal{C}(X\ast A,X\ast A)
\mathcal{C}(Y\ast(X\ast A),C)\times\mathcal{C}(X\ast A,X\ast A)
\mathcal{C}(Y\ast B,C)\times\mathcal{C}(X\ast A,X\ast A)
\mathcal{C}(Y\ast f,{\mathrm{id}})\times\mathcal{C}({\mathrm{id}},{\mathrm{id}})
\mathcal{C}({\mathrm{id}},g\circ(Y\ast f))\times\mathcal{C}({\mathrm{id}},{\mathrm{id}})
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}
\mathcal{C}({\mathrm{id}},g\circ(Y\ast f))
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,X\ast A,Y\ast(X\ast A)}
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,X\ast A,C}
\mathcal{C}({\mathrm{id}},{\mathrm{id}})\times\mathcal{C}({\mathrm{id}},f)

The top square commutes by naturality in
C
of

((\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C})
and the bottom square commutes by
(extra) naturality in
B
of it.
By chasing the appropriate elements as follows

\delta_{X,Y,A}
g\circ(Y\ast f)\circ\delta_{X,Y,A}
(g,f),
({\mathrm{id}_{Y\ast(X\ast A)}},{\mathrm{id}_{X\ast A}})
(g\circ(Y\ast f),{\mathrm{id}_{X\ast A}})
(g,{\mathrm{id}_{X\ast A}})
\mathcal{C}(Y\ast f,{\mathrm{id}})\times\mathcal{C}({\mathrm{id}},{\mathrm{id}})
\mathcal{C}({\mathrm{id}},g\circ(Y\ast f))\times\mathcal{C}({\mathrm{id}},{\mathrm{id}})
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}
\mathcal{C}({\mathrm{id}},g\circ(Y\ast f))
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,X\ast A,Y\ast(X\ast A)}
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,X\ast A,C}
\mathcal{C}({\mathrm{id}},{\mathrm{id}})\times\mathcal{C}({\mathrm{id}},f)

we conclude that
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}(g,f)=g\circ(Y\ast f)\circ\delta_{X,Y,A}
, as desired.
∎

Recall the 2-categories
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich^{\mathrm{r}}(\mathcal{M})}
and
{\mathscr{A}\!ct^{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{oplax}}(\mathcal{M})}

defined in Definition
50
.

Corollary 61

(Cf.
[
Cam18
, Corollary 4.13]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a metatheory.

1.

The 2-functors in Proposition
59

and Proposition
60
restrict to
fully faithful 2-functors

{\mathscr{E}\!nrich^{\mathrm{r}}(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}\qquad{\mathscr{A}\!ct^{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{oplax}}(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}

with the same essential image characterised as follows:
a metamodel
(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})

of
\mathcal{M}
is in the essential image if and only if
for each
X\in\mathcal{M}
and
A,B\in\mathcal{C}
,
the functors

\Phi_{(-)}(A,B)\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}\qquad\Phi_{X}(A,-)\colon\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}

are representable.

2.

The 2-categories
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich^{\mathrm{r}}(\mathcal{M})}
and
{\mathscr{A}\!ct^{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{oplax}}(\mathcal{M})}
are
equivalent.

Proof.

The first clause is immediate from the definition of adjunction.
For instance, an enrichment
(\mathcal{C},{\langle-,-\rangle},j,M)
over
\mathcal{M}

is in
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich^{\mathrm{r}}(\mathcal{M})}
if and only if
for each
A\in\mathcal{C}
,
{\langle A,-\rangle}
is a right adjoint,
which in turn is the case if and only if
for each
X\in\mathcal{M}
and
A\in\mathcal{C}
, the functor

\mathcal{M}(X,{\langle A,-\rangle})\colon\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}

is representable.

The second clause is a direct consequence of the first.
∎

The reader might have noticed that
there is another representability condition not covered by
Propositions
59
and

60
, namely
metamodels
(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})

such that for each
X\in\mathcal{M}
and
B\in\mathcal{C}
,
the functor

\Phi_{X}(-,B)\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}

is representable. They correspond to
right lax actions of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}
on
\mathcal{C}
,
or equivalently, to right oplax actions of
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Extending the definition of enrichment (Definition
33
)
and the 2-category of enrichments (Definition
42
)
to huge monoidal categories, we obtain the following.

Proposition 62
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a metatheory
and
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=([\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{SET}],\widehat{I},\widehat{\otimes})

(see Definition
52
).
The 2-categories
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}
and
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}

are canonically isomorphic.

7.4
{\mathbf{Mod}(-,-)}
as a 2-functor

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a metatheory.
Similarly to the cases of enrichments and oplax actions,
we can view the
{\mathbf{Mod}(-,-)}
construction as a 2-functor
using the 2-category
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}
.
In fact, via the inclusion

{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})},

(16)

the 2-functor
{\mathbf{Mod}(-,-)}
is simply given by the following composite:

{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}
\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
,
inclusion
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}(-,-)

where
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}(-,-)
is the hom-2-functor for
the locally large
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}
.
The inclusion (
16
) identifies
a theory
\mathsf{T}=(T,m,e)
in
\mathcal{M}
with the
metamodel
(\Phi^{(\mathsf{T})},\overline{\phi^{(\mathsf{T})}}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi^{(\mathsf{T})}})

of
\mathcal{M}
in
the terminal category
1
(whose unique object we denote by
\ast
),
defined as follows:

•

the functor
\Phi^{(\mathsf{T})}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\times 1^{\mathrm{op}}\times 1\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
maps
(X,\ast,\ast)
to
\mathcal{M}(X,T)
;

•

the function
(\overline{\phi^{(\mathsf{T})}}_{\cdot})_{\ast}\colon 1\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}(I,T)
maps the unique element of
1
to
e
;

•

for each
X,Y\in\mathcal{M}
, the function

(\overline{\phi^{(\mathsf{T})}}_{X,Y})_{\ast,\ast,\ast}\colon\mathcal{M}(Y,T)\times\mathcal{M}(X,T)\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}(Y\otimes X,T)

maps
(g,f)
to
m\circ(g\otimes f)
.

8
Morphisms of metatheories

In this section, we introduce a notion of morphism between metatheories.
The main purpose of morphisms of metatheories is to provide a
means to compare different notions of algebraic theory.
An example of such comparison
is given in Section
5
,
where we compare clones, symmetric operads and non-symmetric operads.
Recall that the crucial observation used there was the fact that
the
{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(-)}
construction extends to a 2-functor

{\mathscr{E}\!nrich(-)}\colon{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}\longrightarrow\underline{2\mathchar 45\relax\mathscr{C\!A\!T}}.

(17)

Therefore, we want to define morphisms of metatheories with
respect to which
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(-)}
behaves (2-)functorially.

On the other hand, recall from Section
6

that
{\mathscr{A}\!ct_{\mathrm{oplax}}(-)}
is a 2-functor of type

{\mathscr{A}\!ct_{\mathrm{oplax}}(-)}\colon({\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{oplax}}})^{\mathrm{coop}}\longrightarrow\underline{2\mathchar 45\relax\mathscr{C\!A\!T}}.

(18)

Since metamodels unify both enrichments and oplax actions,
we would like to explain both (
17
)
and (
18
) by introducing a
sufficiently general notion of morphism of metatheories.

The requirement to unify both
{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}
and
({\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{oplax}}}^{\mathrm{coop}})

suggests the possibility of using a suitable variant of
profunctors, namely
monoidal profunctors

introduced in Definition
32
.

Definition 63
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I_{\mathcal{M}},\otimes_{\mathcal{M}})
and

\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{N},I_{\mathcal{N}},\otimes_{\mathcal{N}})
be metatheories.
A
morphism of metatheories from
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathcal{N}

is a monoidal profunctor from
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathcal{N}
.

Definition 64
.

We define the bicategory
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{T\!H}}
of metatheories as follows.

•

An object is a metatheory.

•

A 1-cell from
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathcal{N}

is a morphism of metatheories
\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{N}
.
The identity 1-cell on a metatheory
\mathcal{M}
is the hom-functor
\mathcal{M}(-,-)
,
equipped with the evident structure for a morphism of metatheories.
Given morphisms of metatheories

(H,h_{\cdot},h)\colon\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{N}
and

(K,k_{\cdot},k)\colon\mathcal{N}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{L}
,
their composite is
(K\odot H,k_{\cdot}\odot h_{\cdot},k\odot h)\colon\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{L}
where
K\odot H
is the composition of the
profunctors
H
and
K
(Definition
31
), and

k_{\cdot}\odot h_{\cdot}
and
k\odot h
are the evident
natural transformations.

•

A 2-cell from
H
to
H^{\prime}
, both from
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathcal{N}
,
is a monoidal natural transformation

\alpha\colon H\Longrightarrow H^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
.

Similarly to the case of profunctors,
we have identity-on-objects fully faithful pseudofunctors

(-)_{\ast}\colon{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{T\!H}}

and

(-)^{\ast}\colon({\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{oplax}}})^{\mathrm{coop}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{T\!H}}.

In detail, a lax monoidal functor

F=(F,f_{\cdot},f)\colon\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}

gives rise to a morphism of metatheories

F_{\ast}=(F_{\ast},(f_{\ast})_{\cdot},f_{\ast})\colon\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{N}

with
F_{\ast}(N,M)=\mathcal{N}(N,FM)
,
(f_{\ast})_{\cdot}\colon 1\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}(I_{\mathcal{N}},FI_{\mathcal{M}})
mapping the unique element of
1
to

f_{\cdot}\colon I_{\mathcal{N}}\longrightarrow FI_{\mathcal{M}}
,
and
(f_{\ast})_{N,N^{\prime},M,M^{\prime}}\colon\mathcal{N}(N^{\prime},FM^{\prime})\times\mathcal{N}(N,FM)\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}(N^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{N}}N,F(M^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{M}}M))

mapping
g^{\prime}\colon N^{\prime}\longrightarrow FM^{\prime}
and
g\colon N\longrightarrow FM

to
f_{M,M^{\prime}}\circ(g^{\prime}\otimes g)\colon N^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{N}}N\longrightarrow F(M^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{M}}M)
.
Given an oplax monoidal functor

F=(F,f_{\cdot},f)\colon\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N},

we obtain a morphism of metatheories

F^{\ast}=(F^{\ast},(f^{\ast})_{\cdot},f^{\ast})\colon\mathcal{N}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{M}

analogously.

In particular, a
strong
monoidal functor

F\colon\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}

gives rise to both
F_{\ast}\colon\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{N}

and
F^{\ast}\colon\mathcal{N}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{M}
, and
it is straightforward to see that these form an adjunction
F_{\ast}\dashv F^{\ast}
in
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{T\!H}}
.

A morphism of metatheories
H\colon\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{N}

induces a 2-functor

{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(H)}\colon{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{N})}.

Its action on objects is as follows.

Definition 65
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I_{\mathcal{M}},\otimes_{\mathcal{M}})
and

\mathcal{N}=(\mathcal{N},I_{\mathcal{N}},\otimes_{\mathcal{N}})
be metatheories,

H=(H,h_{\cdot},h)\colon\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{N}
be a morphism of metatheories,

\mathcal{C}
be a large category and

\Phi=(\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
be a metamodel of

\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{C}
.
We define the metamodel

H(\Phi)=(\Phi^{\prime},\overline{\phi^{\prime}}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi^{\prime}})

of
\mathcal{N}
on
\mathcal{C}
as follows:

•

The functor

\Phi^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}

maps
(N,A,B)\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}
to the set

\Phi^{\prime}_{N}(A,B)=\int^{M\in\mathcal{M}}H(N,M)\times\Phi_{M}(A,B).

(19)

•

The natural transformation
((\overline{\phi^{\prime}}_{\cdot})_{C}\colon 1\longrightarrow\Phi^{\prime}_{I_{\mathcal{N}}}(C,C))_{C\in\mathcal{C}}

is defined by
mapping the unique element
\ast
of
1
to

[I_{\mathcal{M}}\in\mathcal{M},h_{\cdot}(\ast)\in H(I_{\mathcal{N}},I_{\mathcal{M}}),(\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{C}(\ast)\in\Phi_{I_{\mathcal{M}}}(C,C)]\\
\in\int^{M\in\mathcal{M}}H(I_{\mathcal{N}},M)\times\Phi_{M}(C,C)

for each
C\in\mathcal{C}
.

•

The natural transformation

((\overline{\phi^{\prime}}_{N,N^{\prime}})_{A,B,C}\colon\Phi^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}(B,C)\times\Phi^{\prime}_{N}(A,B)\longrightarrow\Phi^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{N}}N}(A,C))_{N,N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N},A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}}

is defined by mapping a pair consisting of

[M^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]\in\Phi^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}(B,C)
and
[M,x,y]\in\Phi^{\prime}_{N}(A,B)

to

[M^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{M}}M,h_{N,N^{\prime},M,M^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},x),(\overline{\phi}_{M,M^{\prime}})_{A,B,C}(y^{\prime},y)]

for each
N,N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
and
A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}
.

The above construction extends routinely, giving rise to a pseudofunctor

{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(-)}\colon{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{T\!H}}\longrightarrow\underline{2\mathchar 45\relax\mathscr{C\!A\!T}}.

8.1
Comparing different notions of algebraic theory

We now demonstrate how we can compare
different notions of algebraic theory via morphisms of metatheories.

We start with a few remarks on simplification of the action
(Definition
65
) of a morphism of
metatheories
on metamodels, in certain special cases.
Let
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
be metatheories,

H\colon\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{N}

be a morphism of metatheories,

\mathcal{C}
be a large category and

\Phi=(\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
be a metamodel of
\mathcal{M}

in
\mathcal{C}
.

First consider the case where for each
A,B\in\mathcal{C}
,
the functor
\Phi_{(-)}(A,B)\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{SET}}

is representable. This means that
\Phi
is in fact (up to an isomorphism) an
enrichment
{\langle-,-\rangle}
; see Proposition
59
.
In this case,
\Phi_{M}(A,B)
may be written as
\mathcal{M}(M,{\langle A,B\rangle})

and hence the formula (
19
)
simplifies:

\Phi^{\prime}_{N}(A,B)=\int^{M\in\mathcal{M}}H(N,M)\times\mathcal{M}(M,{\langle A,B\rangle})\cong H(N,{\langle A,B\rangle}).

In particular, if moreover
H
is of the form

F_{\ast}\colon\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{N}

for some lax monoidal functor
F\colon\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}
,
then we have

\Phi^{\prime}_{N}(A,B)\cong\mathcal{N}(N,F{\langle A,B\rangle}),

implying that
H(\Phi)=F_{\ast}(\Phi)
is again isomorphic to
an enrichment; indeed, this case reduces to
F_{\ast}({\langle-,-\rangle})

defined in Definition
44
.
Note that, as a special case, for any theory
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{M}

(recall that such a theory is identified with a metamodel of
\mathcal{M}

in the terminal category
1
),

F_{\ast}(\mathsf{T})
is again isomorphic to a theory in
\mathcal{N}
.
Therefore the 2-functor

{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(F_{\ast})}\colon{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{N})}

extends the functor

{\mathbf{Th}(F)}\colon{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{N})}

between the categories of theories induced by
F
, using the well-known fact
that a lax monoidal functor preserves theories (= monoid objects).

Next consider the case where
H
is of the form

G^{\ast}\colon\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{N}

for some oplax monoidal functor
G\colon\mathcal{N}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}
.
In this case
H(N,M)=\mathcal{M}(GN,M)
and the formula
(
19
) simplifies as follows:

\Phi^{\prime}_{N}(A,B)=\int^{M\in\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{M}(GN,M)\times\Phi_{M}(A,B)\cong\Phi_{GN}(A,B).

Suppose now that we have a strong monoidal
functor

F\colon\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}

between metatheories
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
.
On the one hand,
F
induces a functor

{\mathbf{Th}(F)}\colon{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{N})}

between the categories of theories,
which is a restriction of the 2-functor
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(F_{\ast})}
.
On the other hand,
F
induces a 2-functor

{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(F^{\ast})}\colon{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{N})}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}

between the 2-categories of metamodels.
The 2-adjointness
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(F_{\ast})}\dashv{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(F^{\ast})}

yields, for each theory
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{M}

and each metamodel
(\mathcal{C},\Phi)
of
\mathcal{N}
,
an isomorphism of categories

{\mathbf{Mod}(F_{\ast}(\mathsf{T}),(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}\cong{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},F^{\ast}(\Phi)))}.

Observe that
F_{\ast}(\mathsf{T})={\mathbf{Th}(F)}(\mathsf{T})
is the standard action of
a strong monoidal
functor on a theory, and
F^{\ast}(\Phi)
is, in essence,
simply precomposition by
F
.

Now we apply the above argument to some concrete cases.

Example 66
.

Recall from Section
5
, where we have compared clones,
symmetric operads and non-symmetric operads, that there is a chain of lax
monoidal functors

[\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}]
[\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}]
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
.
\mathrm{Lan}_{J}
\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime}}

These functors, being left adjoints in
{\mathscr{M}\!on\mathscr{C\!A\!T}_{\mathrm{lax}}}
, are in fact strong monoidal

[
Kel74b
]
.
Theories are mapped as follows, as noted in Section
5
:

{\mathbf{Th}([\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}])}
{\mathbf{Th}([\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}])}
{\mathbf{Th}([\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}])}.
{\mathbf{Th}(\mathrm{Lan}_{J})}
{\mathbf{Th}(\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime}})}

In this case, the suitable 2-functors between 2-categories of metamodels
can be given either as
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od((\mathrm{Lan}_{J})^{\ast})}
or
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(([J,\mathbf{Set}])_{\ast})}

(and similarly for
J^{\prime}
),
because
(\mathrm{Lan}_{J})^{\ast}\cong([J,\mathbf{Set}])_{\ast}
in
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{T\!H}}
.

Example 67
.

Let us consider the relationship between clones and monads on
\mathbf{Set}
.
The inclusion functor

J^{\prime\prime}\colon\mathbf{F}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Set}

induces a functor

\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime\prime}}\colon[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]\longrightarrow[\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{Set}],

which naturally acquires the structure of a strong monoidal functor.
The essential image of this functor is precisely the
finitary

endofunctors on
\mathbf{Set}
, i.e., those endofunctors preserving filtered colimits.
The functor
{\mathbf{Th}(\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime\prime}})}
maps a clone to a finitary monad on
\mathbf{Set}
,
in accordance with
the well-known correspondence between clones (= Lawvere theories) and finitary
monads on
\mathbf{Set}

[
Lin66
]
.
Between the 2-categories of metamodels, we have a 2-functor

{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od((\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime\prime}})^{\ast})}\colon{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od([\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{Set}])}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od([\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}])}.

The standard metamodel of
[\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{Set}]
in
\mathbf{Set}
(corresponding to the
definition of Eilenberg–Moore algebras) is given by the strict action
described in Example
47
;
in particular, its functor part
\Phi\colon[\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{Set}]^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{Set}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{Set}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
maps
(F,A,B)
to

\mathbf{Set}(FA,B)
.
The metamodel
(\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime\prime}})^{\ast}(\Phi)
of
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
in
\mathbf{Set}
has the
functor part
(\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime\prime}})^{\ast}(\Phi)\colon[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{Set}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{Set}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
mapping
(X,A,B)
to

\displaystyle\mathbf{Set}((\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime\prime}}X)A,B)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Set}\left(\int^{[n]\in\mathbf{F}}A^{n}\times X_{n},B\right)

\displaystyle\cong\int_{[n]\in\mathbf{F}}\mathbf{Set}(X_{n},\mathbf{Set}(A^{n},B))

\displaystyle\cong[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}](X,{\langle A,B\rangle}),

where
{\langle A,B\rangle}\in[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
in the final line is the one in
Example
35
.
Hence
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od((\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime\prime}})^{\ast})}
preserves the standard metamodels
and this way we recover the well-known observation
that the classical correspondence
of clones and finitary monads on
\mathbf{Set}
preserves semantics.

Note that by combining the previous example we obtain the chain

[\mathbf{N},\mathbf{Set}]
[\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Set}]
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
[\mathbf{Set},\mathbf{Set}]
\mathrm{Lan}_{J}
\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime}}
\mathrm{Lan}_{J^{\prime\prime}}

of strong monoidal functors, connecting non-symmetric and symmetric operads
with monads on
\mathbf{Set}
.

Example 68
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a metatheory,
\mathcal{C}
a large category,
and
\ast
a
pseudo
action of
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{C}
.
We obtain a strong monoidal functor

F\colon\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]

(where
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]
is equipped with the composition monoidal structure)
as the transpose of
\ast\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
.
The functor
{\mathbf{Th}(F)}
maps any theory
\mathsf{T}=(T,e,m)
in
\mathcal{M}

to the monad
F(\mathsf{T})=(T\ast(-),e\ast(-),m\ast(-))
on
\mathcal{C}
.
The 2-functor

{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(F^{\ast})}\colon{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od([\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}])}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\mathcal{M})}

maps the standard metamodel
\Phi
of
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]
in
\mathcal{C}

(Example
47
) to the metamodel

F^{\ast}(\Phi)\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}

mapping
(X,A,B)
to

\mathcal{C}((FX)A,B)=\mathcal{C}(X\ast A,B).

Therefore it maps the standard metamodel
\Phi
to the metamodel induced from

\ast
.

As a special case, for a large category
\mathcal{C}
with finite limits and a
cartesian monad
\mathsf{S}
on
\mathcal{C}
,
the standard metamodel for
\mathsf{S}
-operads
(Example
48
) may be related to
the standard metamodel of monads on
\mathcal{C}
,
and models of an
\mathsf{S}
-operad
\mathsf{T}
may alternatively be defined
as Eilenberg–Moore algebras of the monad on
\mathcal{C}
induced from
\mathsf{T}

(as noted in
[
Lei04
]
).

8.2
The universal theory

We conclude this section with another application of
the aforementioned method to compare different notions of algebraic
theory via strong monoidal functors.

Let us fix a large category
\mathcal{C}
for the rest of this section.
As we have already seen,
whenever we are given a metatheory
\mathcal{M}
,
a theory
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{M}
and a
metamodel
\Phi
of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{C}
,
we can consider the category
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
of models and
the associated forgetful functor to
\mathcal{C}
.

We now know that
there are in general multiple choices of the input data

(\mathcal{M},\mathsf{T},\Phi)
generating a given category of models
and the associated forgetful functor.
That is, given metatheories
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
, a strong monoidal functor

F\colon\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}
, a theory
\mathsf{T}
in
{\mathcal{M}}

and a metamodel
\Phi
of
\mathcal{N}
in
\mathcal{C}
,
the triples
(\mathcal{M},\mathsf{T},F^{\ast}(\Phi))
and

(\mathcal{N},F_{\ast}(\mathsf{T}),\Phi)
define canonically isomorphic categories of
models over
\mathcal{C}
.
Let us combine this observation with the well-known concept of universal monoid
object
[
ML98
, Section VII.5]
.

We define the
augumented simplex category
(also known as the

algebraists’ simplex category
)
\Delta_{a}

[
ML98
, Section VII.5]
to be the category of all finite ordinals
(including the empty ordinal
\mathbf{0}
) and monotone functions.
The ordinal with
n
elements
\{0<1<\dots<n-1\}

is denoted by
\mathbf{n}
.
We endow
\Delta_{a}
with the structure of a strict monoidal category
via ordinal sum as monoidal product.
The (terminal) object
\mathbf{1}\in\Delta_{a}
, together with the
unique morphisms
!_{\mathbf{0}}\colon\mathbf{0}\longrightarrow\mathbf{1}
and

!_{\mathbf{2}}\colon\mathbf{2}\longrightarrow\mathbf{1}

define a monoid object, i.e., a theory, in
\Delta_{a}
.
This theory
\mathsf{U}=(\mathbf{1},!_{\mathbf{0}},!_{\mathbf{2}})

is in fact the
universal
theory,
in the sense that for any metatheory
\mathcal{M}

and a theory
\mathsf{T}
therein,
there exists a strong monoidal functor
F\colon\Delta_{a}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}
,
uniquely up to monoidal natural isomorphisms,
which carries
\mathsf{U}
to
\mathsf{T}
.
(The monoidal category
\Delta_{a}
being isomorphic to the PRO of monoids,
this last assertion is just the reformulation of its models in the style of
functorial
semantics.)

Consequently,
given any triple
(\mathcal{M},\mathsf{T},\Phi)
defining a
category of models over
\mathcal{C}
,
we get the strong monoidal functor
F\colon\Delta_{a}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}

corresponding to
\mathsf{T}
(so that
F_{\ast}(\mathsf{U})\cong\mathsf{T}
),
and hence obtain the triple
(\Delta_{a},\mathsf{U},F^{\ast}(\Phi))
which
defines the category of models over
\mathcal{C}
isomorphic to the original one.
So, in fact, whichever triple
(\mathcal{M},\mathsf{T},\Phi)
we choose,
the category of models over
\mathcal{C}
defined by it can be
realised (up to an isomorphism) as the category of models of the universal
theory
\mathsf{U}
,
with a suitable choice of a metamodel of
\Delta_{a}
in
\mathcal{C}

(or equivalently, in light of Proposition
62
,
with a suitable choice of an enrichment of
\mathcal{C}
over
[\Delta_{a}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{SET}]
).
Let us define the category
{\mathbf{MMod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Delta_{a})}
of
metamodels of
\Delta_{a}
in
\mathcal{C}

as the fibre of
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\Delta_{a})}
(thought of as a 2-category over
\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
via
the evident forgetful 2-functor) above
\mathcal{C}\in\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
, namely as the pullback

{\mathbf{MMod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Delta_{a})}
{\mathscr{M}\!\mathscr{M}\!od(\Delta_{a})}
1
\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
forgetful
{\lceil\mathcal{C}\rceil}

in
\underline{2\mathchar 45\relax\mathscr{C\!A\!T}}
(then this pullback is locally discrete).
We have a functor

{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{U},-)}\colon{\mathbf{MMod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Delta_{a})}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}

(20)

mapping a metamodel
\Phi
of
\Delta_{a}
over
\mathcal{C}
to the category of
models
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{U},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
equipped with the forgetful functor
to
\mathcal{C}
.

The open problem of finding an intrinsic characterisation of
the class of forgetful functors
arising in our framework, mentioned in the introduction,
may be phrased as that of
finding intrinsic properties of objects in
{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}

which identify the essential image of
the functor (
20
).
From the definition of category of models
(Definition
55
),
it is immediate that
such forgetful functors are faithful and
amnestic
[
AHS06
, Definition 3.27]

isofibrations,
8
8

8

A functor
F\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}

is an
isofibration
iff for any
A\in\mathcal{A}
and an isomorphism

g\colon FA\longrightarrow B^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}
,
there exists an isomorphism
f\colon A\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}

such that
Ff=g
.

for example.
We note that the functor (
20
)
admits a left adjoint, which maps
(V\colon\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C})\in{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}

to the metamodel
\Phi\colon\Delta_{a}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}

defined as

\Phi_{\mathbf{n}}(A,B)=\int^{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{C}(A,VX_{1})\times\mathcal{C}(VX_{1},VX_{2})\times\dots\times\mathcal{C}(VX_{n},B).

9
Structure-semantics adjunctions

Structure-semantics adjunctions
are a classical topic in categorical
algebra.
They are a family of
adjunctions parametrised by a metatheory
\mathcal{M}

and its metamodel
(\mathcal{C},\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
;
if we fix these parameters, the structure-semantics adjunction is

ideally
of type

{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}
{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}
,
\mathrm{Str}
\mathrm{Sem}
\vdash

(21)

and the functor
\mathrm{Sem}
is essentially
{\mathbf{Mod}(-,(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
.
The idea is that we may regard an object of
{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}
, say

V\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
,
as specifying an additional structure (of a very general kind)
on objects in
\mathcal{C}
, by viewing
\mathcal{A}
as the category of

\mathcal{C}
-objects with the additional structure and
V
as the associated
forgetful functor.
The functor
\mathrm{Str}
then extracts a theory from
V
,
giving the “best approximation” of this additional structure by
structures expressible by theories in
\mathcal{M}
.
Various authors have constructed such adjunctions for a variety of
notions of algebraic theory, most notably for
clones
[
Law63
,
Lin66
,
Isb72
]
and monads
[
Dub70
,
Str72
]
.
There are also some attempts to unify these
results
[
Lin69
,
Ave17
]
.

If we try to work this idea out, however, there turn out to be size-issues or
other problems, and usually we cannot obtain an adjunction of type
(
21
);
we cannot find a suitable functor
\mathrm{Str}
of that type.
To get an adjunction, various conditions on objects in
{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}

were introduced in the literature in order to single out well-behaved
(usually called
tractable
) objects,
yielding a restricted version of (
21
):

{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}
({\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C})_{\mathrm{tr}}
.
\mathrm{Str}
\mathrm{Sem}
\vdash

(22)

Here,
({\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C})_{\mathrm{tr}}
is the full-subcategory of

{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}
consisting of all tractable objects.

In this section, we construct a structure-semantics adjunction for
an arbitrary metatheory and an arbitrary metamodel of it.
Of course, we cannot obtain an adjunction of type (
21
),
for the same reasons that have prevented
other authors from doing so.
However, we shall obtain a modified adjunction by a strategy different from
theirs (and similar to
[
Lin69
,
Ave17
]
):
instead of restricting
{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}
,
we extend
{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}
to
{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}
9
9

9

The monoidal
category
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
is not a metatheory because it is not large.
Extending Definition
21
, by
{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}
we mean
the category of monoids in
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
.

(where
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=[\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{SET}]
is equipped with
the convolution monoidal structure),
and obtain an extended version of (
21
):

{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}^{\mathrm{op}}
{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}
.
\mathrm{Str}
\mathrm{Sem}
\vdash

(23)

We may then obtain known adjunctions of the form (
22
)
for clones and monads,
by suitably restricting (
23
).

9.1
The structure and semantics functors

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
be a metatheory,
\mathcal{C}
be a large
category, and
\Phi=(\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
be a
metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{C}
.
The metamodel
\Phi
enables us to define, for each theory
\mathsf{T}\in{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}
, the category of models
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}

together with the forgetful functor
U\colon{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
.
This construction is functorial, and gives rise to a functor

{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}.

However, as we have remarked in Proposition
62
,
a metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{C}
corresponds to
an enrichment of
\mathcal{C}
over
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
;
hence using
\Phi
we can actually give
the definition of models for any theory (i.e., monoid object) in

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
.
Therefore the previous functor can be extended to

\mathrm{Sem}\colon{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}.

(24)

The category
{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}
is
isomorphic to the category of lax monoidal functors of type

\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
and monoidal natural transformations between
them.
Indeed, an object
(P,e,m)
of
{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}

consists of:

•

a functor
P\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
;

•

a natural transformation
(e_{X}\colon\widehat{I}(X)\longrightarrow P(X))_{X\in\mathcal{M}}
;

•

a natural transformation
(m_{X}\colon(P\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}P)(X)\longrightarrow P(X))_{X\in\mathcal{M}}

satisfying the monoid axioms, and such a data is equivalent to

•

a functor
P\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
;

•

a function
\overline{e}\colon 1\longrightarrow P(I)
;

•

a natural transformation
(\overline{m}_{X,Y}\colon P(Y)\times P(X)\longrightarrow P(Y\otimes X))_{X,Y\in\mathcal{M}}

satisfying the axioms for
(P,\overline{e},\overline{m})
to be a
lax monoidal functor
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
.
We shall use these two descriptions of objects in

{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}

interchangeably.

Let us describe the action of the functor
\mathrm{Sem}
concretely.
For any
\mathsf{P}=(P,e,m)\in{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}
, we define
the category
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{P},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
as follows:

•

An object is a pair consisting of an object
C\in\mathcal{C}
and
a natural transformation

(\xi_{X}\colon P(X)\longrightarrow\Phi_{X}(C,C))_{X\in\mathcal{M}}

making the following diagrams commute for each
X,Y\in\mathcal{M}
:

1
P(I)
\Phi_{I}(C,C)
\overline{e}
\xi_{I}
(\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{C}

P(Y)\times P(X)
P({Y\otimes X})
\Phi_{Y}(C,C)\times\Phi_{X}(C,C)
\Phi_{Y\otimes X}(C,C).
\overline{m}_{X,Y}
\xi_{Y\otimes X}
\xi_{Y}\times\xi_{X}
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{C,C,C}

(25)

•

A morphism from
(C,\xi)
to
(C^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
is a morphism
f\colon C\longrightarrow C^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}
making the following diagram commute for
each
X\in\mathcal{M}
:

P(X)
\Phi_{X}(C,C)
\Phi_{X}(C^{\prime},C^{\prime})
\Phi_{X}(C,C^{\prime}).
\xi_{X}
\Phi_{X}(C,f)
\xi^{\prime}_{X}
\Phi_{X}(f,C^{\prime})

There exists an evident forgetful functor

U\colon{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{P},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
mapping

(C,\xi)
to
C
and
f
to
f
;
the functor
\mathrm{Sem}
maps
\mathsf{P}
to
U
.

We have a canonical fully faithful functor

J\colon{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}

mapping
(T,e,m)\in{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}
to the functor
\mathcal{M}(-,T)

with the evident monoid structure induced from
e
and
m
.
An object
(P,e,m)\in{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}
is in the essential
image of
J

if and only if
P\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
is representable.

Let us describe the left adjoint
\mathrm{Str}
to (
24
).
Given an object
V\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
of
{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}
,
we define
\mathrm{Str}(V)=(P^{(V)},e^{(V)},m^{(V)})\in{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}
as
follows:

•

The functor
P^{(V)}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}

maps
X\in\mathcal{M}
to

P^{(V)}(X)=\int_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\Phi_{X}(VA,VA).

(26)

•

The function
\overline{e^{(V)}}\colon 1\longrightarrow P^{(V)}(I)

maps the unique element of
1
to

((\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{VA}(\ast))_{A\in\mathcal{A}}

\in P^{(V)}(I)
.

•

The
(X,Y)
-th component of the natural transformation

(\overline{m^{(V)}}_{X,Y}\colon P^{(V)}(Y)\times P^{(V)}(X)\longrightarrow P^{(V)}(Y\otimes X))_{X,Y\in\mathcal{M}}

maps
((y_{A})_{A\in\mathcal{A}},(x_{A})_{A\in\mathcal{A}})

to
((\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{VA,VA,VA}(y_{A},x_{A}))_{A\in\mathcal{A}}
.

The monoid axioms for
(P^{(V)},{e^{(V)}},{m^{(V)}})

follow easily from the axioms for metamodels,
and
\mathrm{Str}
routinely extends to a functor of type

{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Theorem 69
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a metatheory,
\mathcal{C}
be a large category and

\Phi=(\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})

be a metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{C}
.
The functors
\mathrm{Sem}
and
\mathrm{Str}
defined above
form an adjunction:

{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}^{\mathrm{op}}
{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}
.
\mathrm{Str}
\mathrm{Sem}
\vdash

Proof.

We show that there are bijections

{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}(\mathsf{P},\mathrm{Str}(V))\cong({\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C})(V,\mathrm{Sem}(\mathsf{P}))

natural in
\mathsf{P}=(P,e,m)\in{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}
and
(V\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C})\in{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}
.

In fact, we show that the following three types of data naturally
correspond to each other.

1.

A morphism
\alpha\colon\mathsf{P}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Str}(V)
in

{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}
; that is, a natural transformation

(\alpha_{X}\colon P(X)\longrightarrow P^{(V)}(X))_{X\in\mathcal{M}}

making the suitable diagrams commute.

2.

A natural transformation

(\xi_{A,X}\colon P(X)\longrightarrow\Phi_{X}(VA,VA))_{X\in\mathcal{M},A\in\mathcal{A}}

making the following diagrams commute for each
A\in\mathcal{A}
and

X,Y\in\mathcal{M}
:

1
P(I)
\Phi_{I}(VA,VA)
\overline{e}
\xi_{A,I}
(\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{VA}

P(Y)\times P(X)
P({Y\otimes X})
\Phi_{Y}(VA,VA)\times\Phi_{X}(VA,VA)
\Phi_{Y\otimes X}(VA,VA).
\overline{m}_{X,Y}
\xi_{A,Y\otimes X}
\xi_{A,Y}\times\xi_{A,X}
(\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{VA,VA,VA}

3.

A morphism
F\colon V\longrightarrow\mathrm{Sem}(\mathsf{P})
in
{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}
;
that is, a functor

F\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{P},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}

such that
U\circ F=V

(
U\colon{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{P},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
is the
forgetful functor).

The correspondence between 1 and 2 is by the universality of ends
(see (
26
)).
To give
\xi
as in 2
without
requiring naturality
in
A\in\mathcal{A}
, is equivalent to give a
function
{\mathrm{ob}(F)}\colon{\mathrm{ob}(\mathcal{A})}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{ob}({\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{P},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))})}

such that
{\mathrm{ob}(U)}\circ{\mathrm{ob}(F)}={\mathrm{ob}(V)}
(see (
25
)).
To say that
\xi
is natural also in
A\in\mathcal{A}

is equivalent to saying that
{\mathrm{ob}(F)}
extends to a functor

F\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{P},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}

by mapping each morphism
f
in
\mathcal{A}
to
Vf
.
∎

9.2
The classical cases

We now show that one can recover the known
structure-semantics adjunctions for clones and monads, by restricting
our version of structure-semantics adjunctions
(Theorem
69
).

In both cases of clones and monads, we shall consider the diagram

{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{\widehat{M}})}^{\mathrm{op}}
{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}
\mathrm{Str}
\mathrm{Sem}
\vdash
{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}^{\mathrm{op}}
({\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C})_{\mathrm{tr}}
\mathrm{Str}^{\prime}
\mathrm{Sem}^{\prime}
\vdash
J
K

in which the top adjunction is the one we have constructed above,
the bottom adjunction is a classical structure-semantics adjunction,
and
J
and
K
are the canonical fully faithful functors
(the precise definition of
({\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C})_{\mathrm{tr}}
will be given below).
We shall prove that the two squares, one involving
\mathrm{Str}
and
\mathrm{Str}^{\prime}
,
the other involving
\mathrm{Sem}
and
\mathrm{Sem}^{\prime}
, commute,
showing that
\mathrm{Str}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathrm{Sem}^{\prime}
) arises as a restriction of

\mathrm{Str}
(resp.
\mathrm{Sem}
).

First, that
K\circ\mathrm{Sem}^{\prime}\cong\mathrm{Sem}\circ J
holds is straightforward,
and this is true as soon as
\mathrm{Sem}^{\prime}
maps any
\mathsf{T}\in{\mathbf{Th}(\mathcal{M})}

to the forgetful functor

U\colon{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
.
Indeed, for any theory
\mathsf{T}=(T,e,m)
in
\mathcal{M}
,

J(\mathsf{T})\in{\mathbf{Th}(\widehat{\mathcal{M}})}
has the underlying object

\mathcal{M}(-,T)\in\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
, and the description of

{\mathbf{Mod}(J(\mathsf{T}),(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
in the previous section coincides with

{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
by the Yoneda lemma.

Let us check that
J\circ\mathrm{Str}^{\prime}\cong\mathrm{Str}\circ K
holds.
10
10

10

This does not
seem to follow formally from
K\circ\mathrm{Sem}^{\prime}\cong\mathrm{Sem}\circ J
,
even if we take into consideration the fact that
J
and
K
are fully
faithful.

For this, we have to review the classical structure functors
and the tractability conditions.

We begin with the case of clones as treated in
[
Lin66
]
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a locally small category with finite powers and
consider the standard metamodel
\Phi
of
[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}]
in
\mathcal{C}

(derived from the enrichment
{\langle-,-\rangle}
in
Example
35
).
An object
V\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}\in{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}
is called

tractable
if and only if for any natural number
n
,
the set
[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}]((-)^{n}\circ V,V)
is small.
Given a tractable
V
,
\mathrm{Str}^{\prime}(V)\in{\mathbf{Th}([\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}])}
has the underlying
functor
|\mathrm{Str}^{\prime}(V)|
mapping
[n]\in\mathbf{F}
to
[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}]((-)^{n}\circ V,V)
.
On the other hand, our formula (
26
)
reduces as follows:

\displaystyle P^{(V)}(X)

\displaystyle=\int_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\Phi_{X}(VA,VA)

\displaystyle=\int_{A\in\mathcal{A}}[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}](X,{\langle VA,VA\rangle})

\displaystyle\cong\int_{A\in\mathcal{A},[n]\in\mathbf{F}}\mathbf{Set}(X_{n},\mathcal{C}((VA)^{n},VA))

\displaystyle\cong\int_{[n]\in\mathbf{F}}\mathbf{Set}\bigg(X_{n},\int_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{C}((VA)^{n},VA)\bigg)

\displaystyle\cong\int_{[n]\in\mathbf{F}}\mathbf{Set}(X_{n},[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}]((-)^{n}\circ V,V))

\displaystyle\cong[\mathbf{F},\mathbf{Set}](X,|\mathrm{Str}^{\prime}(V)|).

It is routine from this to see that
J\circ\mathrm{Str}^{\prime}\cong\mathrm{Str}\circ K
holds.

As for monads, we take as a classical structure-semantics adjunction
the one in
[
Dub70
, Section II. 1]
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a large category and consider the standard metamodel
\Phi

of
[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}]
in
\mathcal{C}
(derived from the standard strict action

\ast
in Example
47
).
An object
V\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}\in{\mathbf{CAT}}/\mathcal{C}

is called
tractable
if and only if the right Kan extension

\mathrm{Ran}_{V}V
of
V
along itself exists.
11
11

11

In fact, in

[
Dub70
, p. 68]

Dubuc defines tractability as a slightly stronger condition.
However, the condition we have introduced above is the one which is used for
the construction of structure-semantics adjunctions in
[
Dub70
]
.

A functor of the form

\mathrm{Ran}_{V}V
acquires a canonical monad structure,
giving rise to the so-called codensity monad of
V
.
For a tractable
V
,
\mathrm{Str}^{\prime}(V)
is defined to be the codensity monad of
V
.
Now let us return to our formula (
26
):

\displaystyle P^{(V)}(X)

\displaystyle=\int_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\Phi_{X}(VA,VA)

\displaystyle=\int_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{C}(XVA,{VA})

\displaystyle\cong[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}](X\circ V,V)

\displaystyle\cong[\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}](X,\mathrm{Ran}_{V}V).

Again we see that
J\circ\mathrm{Str}^{\prime}\cong\mathrm{Str}\circ K
holds.

10
Categories of models as double limits

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a metatheory,
\mathsf{T}
be a theory in
\mathcal{M}
,

\mathcal{C}
be a large category and
\Phi
be a metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
in

\mathcal{C}
.
Given these data, in Definition
55
we have defined—in
a concrete manner—the category
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
of models
of
\mathsf{T}
in
\mathcal{C}
with respect to
\Phi
,
equipped with the forgetful functor

U\colon{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
.

In this section, we give an abstract characterisation of the categories of
models.
A similar result is known for the Eilenberg–Moore category
of a monad; Street
[
Str72
]

has proved that it can be abstractly characterised as the lax limit in

\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
of a certain diagram canonically constructed from the original monad.
We prove that the categories of models in our framework can also be
characterised by a certain universal property.
A suitable language to express this universal property is
that of
pseudo double categories

[
GP99
]
.
We show that the category
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
, together with
the forgetful functor
U
and some other natural data, form a double limit
in the pseudo double category
{\mathbb{PROF}}
of large categories,
profunctors, functors and natural transformations.

10.1
The universality of Eilenberg–Moore categories

Let us begin with quickly reviewing the 2-categorical characterisation in

[
Str72
]
of the Eilenberg--Moore category of a monad on a large
category, in elementary terms.
12
12

12

The main point of the paper

[
Str72
]
is the introduction of the general
notion of Eilenberg–Moore object in a 2-category
\mathscr{B}
via a universal
property and show
that, if exists, it satisfies certain formal properties of Eilenberg–Moore
categories.
However, for our purpose, it suffices to consider the simple case

\mathscr{B}=\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
only. It is left as future work to investigate whether
we can develop a similar “formal theory” from the double-categorical
universal property of categories of models.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a large category and
\mathsf{T}=(T,\eta,\mu)
be a monad on

\mathcal{C}
.
The Eilenberg–Moore category
\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}}
of
\mathsf{T}

is equipped with a canonical forgetful functor
U\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
, mapping an Eilenberg–Moore algebra

(C,\gamma)
of
\mathsf{T}
to its underlying object
C
.
Moreover, there exists a canonical natural transformation
u\colon T\circ U\Longrightarrow U
, i.e., of type

\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}}
\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}}
\mathcal{C}
\mathcal{C}.
U
{\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}}}}
T
U
u

We are depicting
u
in a square rather than in a triangle for later comparison
with similar diagrams in a pseudo double category.
For each
(C,\gamma)\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}}
,
the
(C,\gamma)
-th component of
u
is simply
\gamma\colon TC\longrightarrow C
.
The claim is that the data
(\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}},U,u)
is characterised by a
certain universal property.

To state this universal property, let us define
a
left
\mathsf{T}
-module
to be
a triple
(\mathcal{A},V,v)
consisting of a large category
\mathcal{A}
,
a functor
V\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
and a natural transformation

v\colon T\circ V\Longrightarrow V
,
such that the following equations hold:

The triple
(\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}},U,u)
is then a
universal left

\mathsf{T}
-module
, meaning that it satisfies the following:

1.

it is a left
\mathsf{T}
-module;

2.

for any left
\mathsf{T}
-module
(\mathcal{A},V,v)
,
there exists a unique functor
K\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}}

such that

\hbox to83.53pt{\vbox to67.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.31047pt\lower-32.9889pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

holds,
there exists a unique natural transformation
\sigma\colon K\Longrightarrow K^{\prime}

such that
\theta=U\circ\sigma
, where
K\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}}

and
K^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}}
are the functors
corresponding to
(\mathcal{A},V,v)
and

(\mathcal{A},V^{\prime},v^{\prime})
respectively.

In more conceptual terms, what is asserted is that we have a family of
isomorphisms of categories

\mathscr{C\!A\!T}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}})\cong\mathscr{C\!A\!T}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})^{\mathscr{C\!A\!T}(\mathcal{A},\mathsf{T})}

natural in
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
,
where the right hand side denotes the Eilenberg–Moore category of the
monad
\mathscr{C\!A\!T}(\mathcal{A},\mathsf{T})
; note that

\mathscr{C\!A\!T}(\mathcal{A},-)
is a 2-functor and therefore preserves monads.

As with any universal characterisation, the above property characterises
the triple
(\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{T}},U,u)
uniquely up to
unique isomorphisms.
One can also express this universal property in terms of the standard
2-categorical limit notions, such as lax limit or weighted 2-limit

[
Str76
]
.

10.2
Pseudo double categories

We shall see that our category of models admit a similar characterisation,
in a different setting:
instead of the 2-category
\mathscr{C\!A\!T}
, we will work within
the
pseudo double category

{\mathbb{PROF}}
.
The notion of pseudo double category is due to Grandis and Paré
[
GP99
]
,
and it generalises the classical notion of double category
[
Ehr65
]

in a way similar to the generalisation of 2-categories to bicategories.

Recall that a double category consists of
objects

A
,

vertical
morphisms

f\colon A\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
,

horizontal morphisms

X\colon A\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}B

and
squares

A
B
A^{\prime}
B^{\prime}

\alpha

namely:

•

for each object
A
we have the
vertical identity morphism

{\mathrm{id}_{A}}\colon A\longrightarrow A
;

•

f\colon A\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}\colon A^{\prime}\longrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}

f^{\prime}\circ f\colon A\longrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}
;

These identity and composition operations are required to satisfy several
axioms, such as the unit and associativity axioms for vertical
(resp. horizontal)
identity and composition, as well as the axiom

{\mathrm{id}_{I_{A}}}=I_{\mathrm{id}_{A}}
for each object
A
and
the
interchange law
, saying that whenever we have a configuration
of squares as in

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

\bullet

\alpha

(\beta^{\prime}\otimes\alpha^{\prime})\circ(\beta\otimes\alpha)=(\beta^{\prime}\circ\beta)\otimes(\alpha^{\prime}\circ\alpha)
holds.

Some naturally arising double-category-like structure,
including
{\mathbb{PROF}}
, are
such that whose vertical morphisms are homomorphism-like (e.g., functors)
and whose horizontal morphisms are bimodule-like (e.g., profunctors);
cf.
[
Shu08
, Section 1]
.
However, a problem crops up from the bimodule-like horizontal
morphisms:
in general, their composition is not unital nor associative on the nose.
Therefore such structures fail to form
(strict) double categories, but instead form

pseudo
(or
weak
)
double
categories

[
GP99
,
Lei04
,
Gar06
,
Shu08
]
,
in which horizontal composition is allowed to
be unital and associative up to suitable isomorphism.
13
13

13

In the
literature, definitions of pseudo double category differ as to
whether to weaken horizontal compositions or vertical compositions.
We follow
[
Gar06
,
Shu08
]
and weaken horizontal
compositions, but note that
the original paper
[
GP99
]
weakens vertical compositions.

We refer the reader to e.g.
[
Gar06
, Section 2.1]

for a detailed definition of pseudo double category.

Example 70

(
[
GP99
]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be a bicategory.
This induces a pseudo double category
\mathbb{H}\mathscr{B}
, given as follows:

•

an object of
\mathbb{H}\mathscr{B}
is an object of
\mathscr{B}
;

•

all vertical morphisms of
\mathbb{H}\mathscr{B}
are vertical identity
morphisms;

•

a horizontal morphism of
\mathbb{H}\mathscr{B}
is a 1-cell of
\mathscr{B}
;

•

a square of
\mathbb{H}\mathscr{B}
is a 2-cell of
\mathscr{B}
.

Conversely, for any pseudo double category
\mathbb{D}
,
we obtain a bicategory
\mathscr{H}\mathbb{D}
given as follows:

•

an object of
\mathscr{H}\mathbb{D}
is an object of
\mathbb{D}
;

•

a 1-cell of
\mathscr{H}\mathbb{D}
is a horizontal morphism of
\mathbb{D}
;

•

a 2-cell of
\mathscr{H}\mathbb{D}
is a square in
\mathbb{D}
whose
horizontal source and target are both vertical identity morphisms.

Let us introduce the pseudo double category
{\mathbb{PROF}}
.

Definition 71

(
[
GP99
, Section 3.1]
)
.

We define the pseudo double category
{\mathbb{PROF}}
as follows.

•

An object is a large category.

•

A vertical morphism from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
is a functor

F\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
.

•

A horizontal morphism from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
is
a profunctor
H\colon\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\displaystyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\textstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\longrightarrow$\cr}}}}\mathcal{B}
, i.e.,
a functor
H\colon\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{SET}
.
Horizontal identities and horizontal compositions are the same as
in Definition
31
.

•

A square as in

\mathcal{A}
\mathcal{B}
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
\mathcal{B^{\prime}}
H
G
F
H^{\prime}
\alpha

is a natural transformation of type

\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{A}
\mathcal{B^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
\mathbf{SET}
.
G^{\mathrm{op}}\times F
H^{\prime}
H
\alpha

Observe that
{\mathscr{P\!R\!O\!F}}=\mathscr{H}{\mathbb{PROF}}
,
using the construction introduced in Example
70
.

Given a pseudo double category
\mathbb{D}
,
denote by
\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{op}}
,
\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{co}}
, and
\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{coop}}

the pseudo double categories obtained from
\mathbb{D}
by reversing
the horizontal direction, reversing the vertical direction
and reversing both the horizontal and vertical directions,
respectively.
In the following we shall mainly work within
{\mathbb{PROF}}^{\mathrm{op}}
,
though most of the diagrams are symmetric in the horizontal direction and
this makes little difference.
(In fact, the pseudo double category defined in
[
GP99
, Section 3.1]

amounts to our
{\mathbb{PROF}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, because our convention on the direction of
profunctors differs from theirs.)

10.3
The universality of categories of models

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a metatheory,
\mathsf{T}=(T,e,m)
be a theory in
\mathcal{M}
,

\mathcal{C}
be a large category, and
\Phi=(\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
be a metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
in

\mathcal{C}
.
Recall from Section
7
that in the data of
the metamodel

(\Phi,\overline{\phi}_{\cdot},\overline{\phi})
,
the natural transformations

((\overline{\phi}_{\cdot})_{C}\colon 1\longrightarrow\Phi_{I}(C,C))_{C\in\mathcal{C}}

and

((\overline{\phi}_{X,Y})_{A,B,C}\colon\Phi_{Y}(B,C)\times\Phi_{X}(A,B)\longrightarrow\Phi_{Y\otimes X}(A,C))_{X,Y\in\mathcal{M},A,B,C\in\mathcal{C}},

may be replaced by
the natural transformations

((\phi_{\cdot})_{A,B}\colon\mathcal{C}(A,B)\longrightarrow\Phi_{I}(A,B))_{A,B\in\mathcal{C}}

and

((\phi_{X,Y})_{A,B}\colon(\Phi_{Y}\mathbin{\odot^{\mathrm{rev}}}\Phi_{X})(A,B)\longrightarrow\Phi_{Y\otimes X}(A,B))_{X,Y\in\mathcal{M},A,B\in\mathcal{C}},

respectively.
In this section we shall mainly use the expression of
metamodel via the data
(\Phi,\phi_{\cdot},\phi)
.
The category of models
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
,
henceforth abbreviated as
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}
,
defined in Definition
55
admits a canonical
forgetful functor
U\colon{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

and a natural transformation (a square in
{\mathbb{PROF}}^{\mathrm{op}}
)
u
as in

{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}
\mathcal{C}
\mathcal{C}.
U
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}(-,-)
\Phi_{T}
U
u

Concretely,
u
is a natural transformation

(u_{(C,\xi),(C^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})}\colon{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}((C,\xi),(C^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))\longrightarrow\Phi_{T}(C,C^{\prime}))_{(C,\xi),(C^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}}

whose
((C,\xi),(C^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))
-th component
maps each morphism
f\colon(C,\xi)\longrightarrow(C^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
in

{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}
to the element

\Phi_{T}(C,f)(\xi)=\Phi_{T}(f,C^{\prime})(\xi^{\prime})\in\Phi_{T}(C,C^{\prime})
.
Alternatively, by the Yoneda lemma,
u
may be equivalently given as
a natural transformation

(\overline{u}_{(C,\xi)}\colon 1\longrightarrow\Phi_{T}(C,C))_{(C,\xi)\in{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}}

whose
(C,\xi)
-th component maps the unique element of
1
to

\xi\in\Phi_{T}(C,C)
.

We claim that the triple
({\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})},U,u)

has a certain universal property.

Definition 72
.

We define a
vertical double cone over
\Phi(\mathsf{T})

to be a triple
(\mathcal{A},V,v)
consisting of a large category
\mathcal{A}
,
a functor
V\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
, and a square
v
in

{\mathbb{PROF}}^{\mathrm{op}}
of type

\mathcal{A}
\mathcal{A}
\mathcal{C}
\mathcal{C},
V
\mathcal{A}(-,-)
\Phi_{T}
V
v

satisfying the following equations:

Using this notion, we can state the universal property of
the triple
({\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})},

U,u)
, just as in the case of
Eilenberg–Moore categories.

Theorem 73
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{M},I,\otimes)
be a metatheory,
\mathsf{T}=(T,e,m)
be a
theory in
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{C}
be a large category, and

\Phi=(\Phi,\phi_{\cdot},\phi)
be a metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
in
\mathcal{C}
.
The triple
({\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})},U,u)
defined above is
a universal vertical double cone over
\Phi(\mathsf{T})
,
namely:

1.

it is a vertical double cone over
\Phi(\mathsf{T})
;

2.

for any vertical double cone
(\mathcal{A},V,v)
over
\Phi(\mathsf{T})
,
there exists a unique functor
K\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}
such that

holds, where
K
and
K^{\prime}
are the functors corresponding to
(\mathcal{A},V,v)
and

(\mathcal{A^{\prime}},V^{\prime},v^{\prime})
respectively.

The above statements are taken from the definition of double limit

[
GP99
, Section 4.2]
.

of Theorem
73
.

First, that
({\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})},U,u)
is a vertical double cone over

\Phi(\mathsf{T})
follows directly from the definition of model of
\mathsf{T}

in
\mathcal{C}
with respect to
\Phi
(Definition
55
).

Given a vertical double cone
(\mathcal{A},V,v)
over
\Phi(\mathsf{T})
,
for each object
A\in\mathcal{A}
, the pair
(VA,v_{A,A}({\mathrm{id}_{A}}))
is a

\mathsf{T}
-model in
\mathcal{C}
with respect to
\Phi
,
and for each morphism
f\colon A\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}
,
the morphism
Vf
is a
\mathsf{T}
-model homomorphism from

(VA,v_{A,A}({\mathrm{id}_{A}}))
to
(VA^{\prime},v_{A^{\prime},A^{\prime}}({\mathrm{id}_{A^{\prime}}}))
.
The functor
K\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}

can therefore be given as
KA=(VA,v_{A,A}({\mathrm{id}_{A}}))
and
Kf=Vf
.
The uniqueness is clear.

Finally, given
H
and
\theta
as in the third clause, the equation
(
27
) says that for each
A\in\mathcal{A}
,
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A^{\prime}}

and
x\in H(A,A^{\prime})
, the
morphism
\theta_{A,A^{\prime}}(x)\colon VA\longrightarrow V^{\prime}A^{\prime}

in
\mathcal{C}
satisfies
\Phi_{T}(VA,\theta_{A,A^{\prime}}(x))(v_{A,A}({\mathrm{id}_{A}}))=\Phi_{T}(\theta_{A,A^{\prime}}(x),VA^{\prime})(v^{\prime}_{A^{\prime},A^{\prime}}({\mathrm{id}_{A^{\prime}}}))
;
in other words, that
\theta_{A,A^{\prime}}(x)
is a
\mathsf{T}
-model homomorphism
from
KA
to
K^{\prime}A^{\prime}
.
The square
\sigma
can then be given as the natural transformation
with
\sigma_{A,A^{\prime}}(x)=\theta_{A,A^{\prime}}(x)
.
∎

10.4
Relation to double limits

We conclude this paper by a brief sketch of how the double categorical
universal property (Theorem
73
) of
categories of models in our framework can be expressed
via the notion of double limit
[
GP99
]
, thus connecting our characterisation
to a well-established notion.
An outline of this reduction is as follows.

1.

A theory
\mathsf{T}
in a metatheory
\mathcal{M}
may be equivalently given
as a strong monoidal functor
\mathsf{T}\colon\Delta_{a}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}
,
where
\Delta_{a}
is the augmented simplex category
(cf. Section
8
).

2.

A metamodel
\Phi
of a metatheory
\mathcal{M}

may be identified with a lax double functor

\Phi\colon\mathbb{H}\Sigma(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}})\longrightarrow{\mathbb{PROF}}^{\mathrm{op}}
,
where
\Sigma
turns a monoidal category to the corresponding one-object
bicategory and
\mathbb{H}
turns a bicategory to the corresponding vertically
discrete pseudo double category (see Example
70
).

3.

Therefore given a theory
\mathsf{T}
and a metamodel
\Phi
(in

\mathcal{C}
) of a metatheory
\mathcal{M}
, we obtain a lax double functor

\Phi(\mathsf{T})\colon\mathbb{H}\Sigma(\Delta_{a}^{\mathrm{op}})\longrightarrow{\mathbb{PROF}}^{\mathrm{op}}

as the following composition:

\mathbb{H}\Sigma(\Delta_{a}^{\mathrm{op}})
\mathbb{H}\Sigma(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}})
{\mathbb{PROF}}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
\mathbb{H}\Sigma(\mathsf{T}^{\mathrm{op}})
\Phi

Theorem
73
may then be interpreted as
establishing that
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},\mathcal{C})}
is (the apex of) the double limit
of
\Phi(\mathsf{T})
in the sense of
[
GP99
]
.

We refer the reader to
[
Fuj18
, Section 5.4]

for a somewhat more detailed sketch.

Corollary 74
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a metatheory,
\mathsf{T}
be a theory in
\mathcal{M}
,

\mathcal{C}
be a large category and
\Phi
be a metamodel of
\mathcal{M}
in

\mathcal{C}
.
The category
{\mathbf{Mod}(\mathsf{T},(\mathcal{C},\Phi))}
of models of
\mathsf{T}

in
\mathcal{C}
with respect to
\Phi
is the apex of
the double limit of the lax double functor
\Phi(\mathsf{T})
.
